6 Movies From the Year 2020 That Are 10/10 Masterpieces

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Feel free to connect with him or check out his work. He's everywhere — Upwork, YouTube, Spotify, SoundCloud, Collider, LinkedIn, Instagram.

Back in 2020, when lockdown shrank life down to repetitive loops like scrolling through a phone half-asleep, reheating tea for the third time, and losing track of what day it was, movies became an anchor. A handful of films stood out because of the vivid moments they left behind. A scene of someone running through the woods, a quiet game of cards with a grandmother, or an entire room belting out a song brought a sense of life back into the room long after the credits rolled.

That is what great cinema does when everything else is on pause. It stays alive in the smaller moments, like a look across a dinner table, a track to hunt down on Spotify, or a decision that feels open to debate forever. Those lasting details are what set this ranking. The number one pick in this list is simply the movie worth putting on again tonight, even with the ending spoiled by memory.

In Wolfwalkers Robyn (Honor Kneafsey) begins convinced she can do her father’s (Sean Bean) work by carrying a crossbow into the woods to kill wolves. The Lord Protector (Simon McBurney) keeps the town behind its walls, and Robyn’s father has been hired to hunt the animals outside them. Then she meets Mebh (Eva Whittaker), a girl who runs barefoot through the forest and becomes a wolf while asleep. Their friendship is not instant softness. Mebh is furious, suspicious, and busy trying to find her mother, Moll MacTíre (Maria Doyle Kennedy), who is trapped in wolf form.

Robyn’s choices become harder in specific ways as she must hide Mebh, question what her father has told her, and face the fact that the hunt threatens the people she wants to protect. The animation makes the town’s straight streets and the forest’s movement feel different to watch, but the most memorable moment is plain. A child sees a frightened mother in a cage and knows the adults are wrong. The rescue gives Robyn courage. But, it does not make her suddenly fearless.

At first, Jake’s (Jesse Plemons) girlfriend (Jessie Buckley) is rehearsing a breakup in her own head while Jake drives her through snow to meet his parents. It is already an unbearable car ride because he keeps talking, changes the radio, and misses her cues while she cannot decide what she means when she imagines ending things. At the farm, his mother (Toni Collette) and father (David Thewlis) age and change between visits, and dinner becomes a test of whether she belongs there. She notices the basement door and does not go down. Later, she does.

I’m Thinking of Ending Things refuses to let anyone settle on who this woman is or what happened. At the high school, the janitor (Guy Boyd) cleans hallways and watches a student production of Oklahoma!, while the film ties him to an older version of Jake. At the barn, they find a pig covered in maggots. Anyone watching leaves wanting to rewind the opening just to hear how much their car conversation has already told them. Even the small changes in her name feel like clues missed while listening to Jake.

Small details stand out the most, such as Soon-ja teaching David Go-Stop, their walk down to the creek where she plants minari, and his resentment giving way to affection. When her stroke leaves her confused, she accidentally starts a fire that destroys Jacob’s crop storage. Nobody has the energy for a neat apology. Later, David runs after her when she tries to leave and guides her back home. Minari lets the family be tired, proud, frightened, and loving in the same scene, making every change in David’s face feel believable.

The Father keeps changing the room while Anthony (Anthony Hopkins) insists that the space is familiar. A daughter named Anne (Olivia Colman) says she is moving to Paris, but later, a man named Paul (Rufus Sewell), whom Anthony does not recognize, is living in the flat and says he is Anne’s husband. Anthony searches for his watch, questions the people caring for him, and tries to catch the lie that will explain why his life no longer adds up. That disorientation surfaces through the smallest interruptions, like a face replaced, furniture shifted, or a conversation repeated with one detail changed.

Anthony reacts with sharp irritation when people correct him, shifts to charm when he thinks he holds the upper hand, and collapses into terror when he cannot place his own daughter. Right before the credits roll, he cries for his mother. He asks to go home. Unraveling which version of the flat is real no longer matters. Someone just needs to stay with him. Someone needs to answer gently.

Before Martha (Amarah-Jae St. Aubyn) arrives, people are carrying chairs, choosing records, cooking, and doing each other’s hair. The room fills. Couples dance close, friends shout across the music, and plates get passed along. Franklyn (Micheal Ward) notices Martha, and they flirt and dance, then slip away briefly from the crowd.

The most memorable moment centers on Janet Kay’s Silly Games. The DJ lets the song play, then the crowd sings so loudly that the track almost disappears. People who have been dancing in pairs now sing together, still shoulder to shoulder, and nobody rushes them to move on. That is the pleasure of Lovers Rock, where a few hours feel important because the people in the room make them so. When Bammy (Daniel Francis-Swaby) sexually assaults Cynthia (Ellis George) in another room, the evening changes, and the others respond with anger and care. The film does not pretend the party makes the assault less serious. Standing at the edge of that room for just one song would be enough.

Nomadland follows Fern (Frances McDormand), whose home is a van, and she has learned the small maintenance tasks that keep it usable, such as finding work, parking overnight, keeping her belongings in order, and deciding what she can carry. After the gypsum plant closes in Empire, Nevada, she takes seasonal work at an Amazon warehouse and meets fellow travelers Linda May (Linda May) and Swankie (Charlene Swankie).

The film does not treat every day as freedom. Fern’s hands are tired after work, she has to find a bathroom, fix what breaks, and decide whether to accept Dave’s (David Strathairn) invitation into his family life. When she visits his son, the baby is there, the house is warm, and Fern could stay. However, she leaves. Later, she returns to Empire and walks through the empty house where she lived with Bo before going back to her van. That choice hurts because Dave is asking her into a real family, and she cares about him. Fern’s life is hers, and the film lets anyone feel what it costs her to keep choosing it.

Steve Herbert: Just when we thought it couldn't get any worse, Crawley Town hit a new low

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The most embarrassing performance and result since we became a football league club in 2011.

As a football club we have hit a new low, when we already thought sitting bottom of the entire football league couldn’t get any worse, we end up being out fought and outplayed by a team who plays at the same level as Three Bridges.

Did you know with an ad-lite subscription to SussexWorld, you get 70% fewer ads while viewing the news that matters to you.

Let that sink in for a minute… a team who plays in a stadium not much different to Jubilee Field has just knocked us out of the FA Cup first round in front of a national television audience.

Losing last season at Boreham Wood at the same stage was bad, but this is next level stuff.

Only this club could produce a result like this one. Four hundred and seventy six Reds fans gave up a whole Remembrance Sunday to attend this horror show in Dorset. Wimborne is probably one of the trickiest places to get too, especially by public transport. It’s quicker to get to Manchester and Liverpool by train than it is to get to the South West. Yet we take the largest away following of the season, and the players let us down like that. Absolutely disgusting.

After talking up all the positivity of the last two league results against Tranmere & Oldham, it’s such a shame that the team have let Kevin Blackwell down so spectacularly. All I can say is thank god the Rotherham game has been postponed. At least it saves us another wasteful day out.

Where do we go from here? Who knows. All I know is if I was one of those players who have let the club down massively and embarrassed every fan who attended and those that watched at home, then a reaction needs to be seen at home to Fleetwood Town in the shape of a performance and a win.

French team: Zinedine Zidane reacts to the tragic death of Jesús de Miguel

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

On the sidelines of the announcement of his second list at the head of the Blues, Zinédine Zidane, who also summoned Pablo Pagis for the first time, was also questioned about the tragic death of the Spanish striker Jesús de Miguel, victim of a violent shock on a football field.

“I don’t want to get into that, I’m sad and extremely touched, it gives me goosebumps, we don’t want to see that on a football field, that’s just the message I want to give. I’m especially sad for the family, we don’t want to see that. I hope we never see that again.”he said. As a reminder, CD Tenerife announced this Tuesday the death of Jesús de Miguel, victim of cardiorespiratory arrest after a violent shock during the match against Andorra.

Pokémon Debuts New Charizard Design Perfect For Kanto Fans

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Pokémon has its fair share of fan-favorites that just about everyone seems to love. This includes Pikachu and the Eeveelutions, but it also includes Charmander's final evolution, Charizard. From Ash's Charizard in the anime to one of the most valuable Pokémon cards of all time, Charizard has made a name for itself as one of the most popular Kanto Pokémon. And over the years, we've seen Pokémon shake up Charizard's design in new and interesting ways.

It also happens to be Pokémon's 30th anniversary this year, which means Charizard and a lot of other Gen 1 Pokémon are being featured in new collabs and on different kinds of merchandise. It's not just merchandise, though, as we've seen FIreRed and LeafGreen release on Switch and Switch 2, and a number of Kanto Pokémon heavily promoted in Pokémon Champions and Pokémon GO throughout 2026.

From two Mega Evolutions to a Shiny variant and more, there are lots of Charizard designs out there. Now, fans have a chance to show off their love for Charizard just in time for the holiday season.

Available at Pokémon Center, "Charizard Red & Green Cardigan" offers up a brand-new design for the beloved Fire-type from the Gen 1 games. This is a part of Pokémon Center's exclusive new holiday collection, which is why there's a red and green scheme here. In addition to having a large Charizard on the back of the sweater, there's also a patch featuring Charizard's head on the front, so both sides of the cardigan represent the classic Pokémon.

The Charizard Red & Green Cardigan is available in adult sizes from XS to 4XL, and none of those sizes are sold out at the time of this writing. If you're looking to represent Charizard this holiday season, you'll need to shell out $59.99 (plus tax) for this item. However, Pokémon Center offers free shipping on orders of $20 or more, so you won't have to worry about paying anything extra on that front. The site has been closed down for maintence for a week, though, so it will hopefully be restored soon.

This Charizard cardigan is just one of many items released on Pokémon Center for Holiday 2026. The current collection is offering up everything from an adorable Espeon ornament to holiday-themed puzzles featuring fan-favorites like Mew and Pikachu. Some items from previous years' holiday collections are also available, and this includes past plushes, snow globes, and winter village set pieces. There's also a new Mew tree-topper being offered this year, which looks pretty awesome.

If you're looking to get this Charizard cardigan, or any of the other holiday items, you'll need to head over to Pokémon Center, since this merchandise is exclusive to that official storefront. Considering how popular Charizard is, it's great to see it getting a cool new design for the holidays.

If you're a fan of Kanto (and, really, who isn't?), then be sure to check out the other Gen 1 Pokémon getting their own sweaters this year. And if you prefer other Pokémon games, then fan-favorites from later generations, like Espeon and Munchlax, are also featured on quite a few items.

Pokémon is a Japanese multimedia franchise created by Satoshi Tajiri and Ken Sugimori, launched by Nintendo, Game Freak, and Creatures in 1996. Centered on fictional creatures called Pokémon, the series spans video games, trading cards, animation, films, merchandise, and competitive events. Players assume the role of Trainers who capture, battle, and trade Pokémon. It is one of the highest-grossing entertainment franchises in history.

Man United latest: Michael Carrick responds to uncertain future after unbelievable comeback

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Manchester United 4-4 Aston Villa: Carrick’s side twice fell apart, either side of another comeback, and will spend the international break 19th

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Michael Carrick said he still expects to be working with Manchester United over the international break and insisted he will not walk away after his players scored two stoppage-time goals to rescue an extraordinary 4-4 draw against Aston Villa in the Premier League.

The visitors appeared on course for a comfortable win after restoring their two-goal advantage in a crazy game Old Trafford, but late goals from Joshua Zirkzee and Patrick Dorgu saw United pull off their second great escape of the week after Tuesday’s incredible comeback victory against Roma.

The result still leaves United in the Premier League’s bottom three, however, and they will spend the international break 18th in the table before visiting rivals Liverpool. United have won just once in their first 10 in the Premier League, but Carrick was still applauded off the pitch by the United fans.

“We haven't won enough games, but I expect to move forward and keep working on things that we've been working on and showing a lot of the good in the game today,” Carrick said. “We need to tighten up on a few mistakes and win football matches.”

Villa boss Unai Emery was hugely frustrated to see his side twice throw away a two-goal lead, and stormed down the tunnel before full-time after Dorgu’s equaliser. Villa were unhappy that United goalkeeper Senne Lammens appeared to prevent Zion Suzuki from catching the ball before Dorgu headed in.

Follow reaction from Manchester United v Aston Villa in the Premier League, below

Belief there could be a comeback like against Roma did not last long. Diogo Dalot’s sloppy challenge on Alysson gave Villa a penalty that John McGinn coolly slotted home three minutes into the second half.

Zion Suzuki brilliantly stopped a curling Cunha effort shortly after a mooted walkout failed to materialise, with Fernandes twice trying his luck as Kobbie Mainoo and Amad Diallo threatened.

Amad continued to prove a nuisance and United’s hard work was rewarded just before half-time.

Tielemans’ low strike from just inside the box beat Suzuki, whose appeals that his view was impeded by Bryan Mbeumo were immaterial as the forward was onside.

But then Tuesday’s matchwinner Matheus Cunha to draw a foul from Boubacar Kamara in the box. Bruno Fernandes scored after the VAR ratified the on-field call, with the 16th-minute penalty sparking the best Premier League display from United since their solitary domestic win back in August.

The mood darkened further in the fourth minute. Manchester United failed to deal with a free-kick and Alysson’s strike was spilled by Senne Lammens, with Nicolas Jackson sweeping the ball into an empty net. “You’re not fit to wear the shirt” bellowed home fans as Villa teased Carrick about his job prospects.

The change Manchester United fans really want goes far beyond the man in the dugout, as highlighted by the green and gold balloons that filled the air around a banner that read ‘we don’t want a new stadium, we want new owners’.

The furious chants against the Glazers, Ratcliffe and director of football Jason Wilcox went up a notch after Unai Emery’s men capitalised on hodgepodge defending in the first minute.

The ball was played wide for Alysson and Dalot allowed him space to hit a first-time strike through Senne Lammens’ legs.

Manchester United have now gone five consecutive games where they have gone 2-0 down. This was the second time, after Roma in midweek, where they avoided defeat.

Manchester United will visit Liverpool in their first game after the international break and will do so while sitting in the Premier League’s bottom three.

They then play Brentford at home, Newcastle away, Coventry at home and Crystal Palace away before taking on Arsenal at the Emirates in their last game before Christmas.

United did manage to win at Anfield last season...

Manchester United are now guaranteed to spend the next 15 days in the Premier League relegation zone.

According to Opta, it’s the longest run of days in the bottom three since September-October 1986.

Asked whether United are in a relegation fight this season, Michael Carrick said: “We’re in November.

“I don’t like this situation where we are and where we’re at. But we can do something about it."

Manchester United have now conceded 30 goals in all competitions this season. According to Opta, no other side in Europe’s top-five league have conceded more.

On his side’s defensive record, Carrick said: “It’s just moments that we need to tighten up on. Individual moments where we’re giving teams goals that we don’t normally get. If we tighten up that, there’s a lot of good in that performance. I thought we should have won the game. But conceding the gaols we are is not helping us at all.

“No goals are good goals to concede. But there are ones you can do a bit more about. There are the ones we gave away today. We have got to tighten up, it’s pretty obvious to say that. It’s giving us too much to do in games. But I thought we did a lot and feel that we should have won the game."

Roy Keane felt Manchester United deserved credit for fighting back to claim a point against Aston Villa but lambasted their defending.

Speaking on Sky Sports, former United midfielder Keane said: “I think we’ve said previously there have been question marks over this team but they’re showing a bit of fight, a bit of desire and, as bad as the game was from a footballing point of view, they kept going to the end.

“So, on the one hand, you say dreadful to give up four goals at home, but they kept going, so we have to give them a little bit of praise.

“In terms of the bigger picture, it’s that rollercoaster of watching United. Some bits were so, so bad. Defensively the way it started, the start of the second half, but they found a way to get a point in a game where they looked dead and buried.

“Still nowhere near good enough to try and get towards the middle of the table.”

Reset your joints and build a more mobile body in 5 moves

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A mobility flow means piecing exercises together so that they form a sequence. This allows you to find more of a flow state, like in yoga or Pilates. It feels rhythmic and calming and can help you tune into your breathing, which is great for resetting your nervous system.

It's also one of my favorite ways to make flexibility and movement feel more inviting, and this five-move routine will help you feel more open, strong, and mobile in your shoulders, hips, spine, back, and hamstrings. You only have five exercises to learn, and you can do them back-to-back in the sequence below.

Grab one of the best yoga mats and get started.

Moving in one long flow helps you work with your breath and feel more like a dance, letting you switch off and enjoy how your body feels as it moves. It should also help reset your joints and spine and find better flexibility in your shoulders and hamstrings.

Perform several rounds, sticking to 6-8 reps per move per side to keep things dynamic.

During each exercise, think to yourself: "Am I using my end range of motion, or could I move more deeply into each position?" If you repeat for several rounds, pause at the top and bottom of each pose and reach deeply into your joints and muscles as you warm up.

Get instant access to breaking news, the hottest reviews, great deals and helpful tips.

I want to highlight two exercises in particular.

Cat-cow is typically used as a spinal mobilizer, and it helps open the front and back body; the back body stretches during "cat" (upper back rounding), and the front body stretches during "cow" (stomach dropping toward the mat).

Downward dog stretches the arms, shoulders, chest and upper back, opening the upper body as you press through your hands and lift your hips up and back. As you draw your heels down, notice a stretch along the lower back, glutes, hamstrings and calves. Press through your hands to help activate your shoulders, then lift the hips as you move your heels.

Take your time and try to enjoy it, and of course, let us know how you get on!

During each exercise, think to yourself: 'Am I using my end range of motion, or could I move more deeply into each position?'

As Podcasters Become Cultural Juggernauts, Brands Should Probably Tune In

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

On a recent episode of Mary Beth Barone and Benito Skinner’s podcast, Ride, the hosts paused their conversation to talk about each other’s ’fits. “What is this kinda top?” Skinner asks, to which Barone replies declaring it vintage Miu Miu. Skinner goes on to cosplay an OOTD video, running through his full outfit: society archive T-shirt “vintage, of course”, vintage Ralph Lauren Polo shorts, and flip flops by The Row. Ride is not a fashion podcast with a capital-F, but this moment underscores why podcasts today — now largely streamed as videos — are becoming important platforms for fashion.

Brands are starting to take note. Skinner regularly sits front row at Prada, and has shot campaigns or content for Tory Burch, J.Crew and Ralph Lauren. Barone has attended the Tory Burch show for the last three seasons. And on the podcast, their brand deals include workout label Tonal, hair brand K18, and Bobbi Brown’s Jones Road.

And it’s not just Ride. During the Spring/Summer 2027 men’s and women’s seasons, podcasters were a front row fixture, including Therapuss’s Jake Shane at Calvin Klein and Dior Men, In Your Dreams host Owen Thiele at Louis Vuitton, and How Long Gone host Chris Black, who’s become a regular at Copenhagen Fashion Week, sometimes alongside co-host Jason Stewart.

In Your Dreams host and Adults star Owen Thiele attended Louis Vuitton SS27 in September.

It makes sense. The global podcast industry generated an estimated $9.2 billion in sales last year, up 23% versus 2024, largely based on a surge in video content, per Bloomberg. Apple and Spotify were once the primary podcasting platforms, but now major streamers including Netflix, Hulu, Tubi, and Disney+ are entering the space. Roughly 81% of weekly podcast listeners now tune into video, either on streaming platforms or YouTube. Video helps podcasters grow their audience: in the age of clipping, many young people will discover new podcasters after seeing the show clipped on TikTok or Reels.

“Over the last three years, the definition of a podcast has changed entirely,” says Rebecca Steinberg, creator agent at United Talent Agency (UTA), who represents Therapuss with Shane, Good Guys with Josh Peck and Ben Soffer, among others. “The way audiences consume podcasts has shifted, and by extension, so has the way the industry defines and categorizes the format.”

With more podcasts signing streaming deals and launching video and live events, the medium is becoming more visual, driving its growth and creating an opportunity for fashion to tap in. But of course, there’s a balance to strike. As these beloved talents build major cultural resonance, it’s important they maintain the credibility and community they’ve cultivated.

The podcast ad read (where the host reads pre-records ads for the break) is the most traditional way for brands to tap into the market. And for the most beloved of podcasters, these reads, often featuring an in-joke or a tongue-in-cheek admission, can be compelling on their own. (In fact, podcast ads deliver the highest ad performance of any brand channel.) “Because people choose to consume podcasts [rather than being bombarded with content on the feed], their recommendations carry more weight,” Steinberg says. “Podcast and creator audiences are incredibly sticky, and that trust creates a unique opportunity for brands to reach potential buyers.”

“Podcasters’ cultural power has grown significantly in recent years,” says Marissa Hurwitz, talent agent at WME, who represents Ride’s Skinner and Barone, alongside other podcasters like Paige DeSorbo (Giggly Squad), Tinx (It’s Me, Tinx), Alaina Urquhart and Ashleigh Kelley (Morbid), Bailey Sarian (Dark History), and Rayna Greenberg and Ashley Hesseltine (Girls Gotta Eat). “It was previously a side door for talents while they worked on other projects. But now, podcasting is the main event. They’ve gone from being niche voices to [the talents] audiences trust the most.”

A.J. Leone is a UTA creators agent who’s worked in the podcast division for nine years. His client roster includes Bowen Yang and Matt Rogers (Las Culturistas), Emma Chamberlain (Anything Goes), Jonathan Van Ness (Getting Better), and Brittany Broski (The Broski Report and Royal Court). “Brands of all kinds want to tap into podcasts today because of the long-form format where the audience is captivated,” he says. “The podcast audience is not just passively scrolling on TikTok. They’re clicking play on YouTube, on their podcast player, on Netflix, and they’re choosing to listen or watch for 45-60 minutes. As a result, a brand partnership that’s part of a podcast feels more authentic; when listening, people feel like they’re on FaceTime with a friend.”

Bowen Yang and Matt Rogers of Las Culturistas hosted their fifth Culture Awards in May of this year. The event was broadcasted by Bravo and Peacock.

As our feeds become increasingly filled with fleeting, transitory connections, and taste continues to convert into a valued commodity, podcasters provide a trusted barometer on what culture people should tune into — from books to movies to songs — or they function as a trusty friend, with whom audiences share in-jokes and a specific vernacular that feels familiar each time they listen. Some have even gone as far as to conceive tongue-in-cheek, IRL award ceremonies, from the Las Culturistas Culture Awards to How Long Gone’s The Goneies, which launched this year, awarding Barone as It-girl of the Year, signifying their role as arbiters of taste, even if it’s esoteric.

So, as the value of taste rises, and audiences seek intellectual influencers who enrich their knowledge rather than serve them slop, podcasters are primed to capture brand marketing revenue from traditional creators.

Mary Beth Barone regularly attends the Tory Burch show at NYFW.

“Established podcasters should be understood as brands in their own right, and an awards show is the clearest sign of that. It’s not a side project, it’s the brand’s IP expanding into the exact territory traditional cultural figures already operate in,” says Parisa Parmar, strategist at culture and entertainment marketing agency Attachment. “Sustained conversation on relevant topics positions a host as a cultural barometer over time, rather than a one-off voice. There’s a genuine appetite for an unfiltered point of view, and it’s fueling what is known as the ‘credibility economy’, where having a genuine take is a form of social power or capital.”

Podcaster relatability is what moves the needle. “Culturally, we have a strange relationship with expertise. We want those people who don’t seem like they’re talking down to us,” says Annie Corser, pop culture analyst at Stylus. “Podcasters have some more inbuilt credibility, so we allow them to be tastemakers, it’s different to other types of media like film or TV in that it is by and large non-fiction. So we’re going there for information.”

This is compelling for partners, adds WME’s Hurwitz. “You’re really getting to work with people who haven’t just necessarily also grown up and been like, ‘I want to be a star,’” she says. “It’s people who have these really fascinating niche interests that they use their podcasts to share.”

The visual nature of modern podcasts is changing how podcasters work with brands, experts agree. Las Culturistas — the growing pop culture podcast hosted by Yang and Rogers — signed a new deal with Disney+ and Hulu on October 15, to stream the pod on both platforms. Leaning into the visual side of the show, the duo launched a new video set over the summer, which has allowed for more product placements and brand interactions, says UTA agent Leone. “Fashion hasn’t tapped into the podcast space as significantly as other categories,” he adds. “But it’s only a matter of time, especially with the added visual component.”

Shane — who is increasingly booking major talent interviews — signed a deal with Netflix in January to stream his episodes on the platform. “Bringing Therapuss to Netflix was one inflection point that signaled platforms traditionally associated with TV and film see real value in licensing and distributing creator-produced shows that originated on social and audio platforms,” Steinberg says. “Hollywood is seeing that these creators are the new frontier of entertainment — backing them with bigger budgets, new distribution, and the ability to story tell on a different stage.”

Therapuss host Jake Shane attended Calvin Klein SS27; he also signed a major deal with Alo in the summer, as brands begin to understand the power of his hyper-engaged community.

This summer, Alo tapped Shane as an ambassador, launching a workout series with him on the Alo app, after he spent three years training at the Alo Wellness Club in LA (and posting the difficult journey). “It’s clear to the audience that there is a true relationship there, and I think the customer can just feel it and see it, and that’s why they connect with the brand more organically,” said Alo CEO Benedetta Petruzzo after the launch.

Podcasters today are such prominent cultural figures that many of them are already being styled and photographed for events, Hurwitz says. “The audiences are so loyal and engaged in a way that translates to real purchasing power. People with a podcast business like Benito, Mary Beth, Hannah Berner, Paige Desorbo, Tinx, you see all of them showing up at these fashion week events because fans truly want to see where they’re going, what they’re wearing, who they’re wearing, and how can they get their hands on it.”

The evolution of podcast video is “irrefutably big”, Stylus’s Corser says. But the next avenue for growth, she shares, is IRL podcast recordings and events, presenting more brand activation opportunities.

Barone and Skinner completed a Ride live tour last year, where they held IRL versions of the podcast across the US and Europe in sold-out venues. People turned up in T-shirts with in-jokes from the pod, Hurwitz says. “Baronies” are also increasingly staging their own IRL events, having connected via Instagram fan accounts and group chats, presenting a hyper-engaged audience for brands to tap into via sponsorship. “People have chosen to go and see people that they think are friends, and then the assumption is these friends of mine will have chosen brands really carefully, so I’ll pay attention to them,” Corser says. “People want brands to help them get out of the house.”

Owen Thiele of In Your Dreams and Benito Skinner of Ride at the WYouth launch in Los Angeles.

There’s more and more brands that want to be part of that real-life podcast moment, says Leone. Podcaster Heather McMahan of Absolutely Not even staged a live event on a cruise. “Her fans are willing not just to go on the road with her, but are willing to go on a boat and do a whole cruise with her,” he adds. “So it’s really the community that they build.”

Of course, the brands showing up on video or at live events need to align with the podcasters’ actual tastes. “One of the things that we really focus on at WME is trying to super serve [the audience] and ensure that we’re protective of the podcaster,” talent agent Hurwitz says. Whether it’s on the pod or off, “we never want to do anything with the brand that would jeopardize the incredible relationship and community that they’ve built with the audience.”

Hurwitz points to a recent episode of Ride as an example of how to work with brands that fit. “One ad was for Squarespace, which Mary Beth used to build her website,” she explains. “Another of the ads was for Nocd, so Benny spoke about the fact he was diagnosed with OCD. It’s about aligning partnerships with who they are as people.”

Alignment is “the biggest thing”, Steinberg concurs. “Brands need to let podcasters talk about a product in their own voice, and trust that they know their audience best and what will be entertaining, informative and actually resonate. If a brand is overly prescriptive about the creative, it can be difficult because there’s going to be misalignment. Creators have a direct relationship with their audience, and they know what works.”

Live events allow brands to be part of a podcast community without interrupting the pod during an ad break. “Meaningful alignment means going where the fans already gather outside the show itself, and adding something rather than inserting something,” Parmar says. “That might mean a live event built around the podcast’s community rather than its content, a physical product collaboration that only makes sense because of who the host is, or simply showing up to genuinely help solve a problem that community already has.”

Based on their growing cultural power, podcast talents are expanding their careers across different fields, presenting more opportunities as they grow. Yang (who was previously a regular on Saturday Night Live) is currently starring in Oh, Mary! on Broadway, Thiele acts in TV series Adults, while Skinner wrote and starred in series Overcompensating on Prime, which also features Barone and is currently filming season two. The podcast is a crucial platform for talents to build a closer community, boosting their external projects, Hurwitz says. “Now, we can take a podcast business and build it into many other areas. Whether that is TV, is it a book? Is it a tour? Is it a venture deal? Is it more brand deals? That’s really helped differentiate some of the podcasters we represent.”

Is Jensen Huang’s job on the line? Yale’s Jeffrey Sonnenfeld estimates it’s “about a 50-50”

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Nvidia’s board announced Sunday that it’s launching an independent investigation after CEO Jensen Huang told staff he was unfaithful to his wife. But this isn’t a case of just another CEO having an extramarital affair. The romantic interest is Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, whom U.S. officials believe is a Chinese spy who was feeding information back to Beijing. In short, the stakes reach well beyond Nvidia, given how many tech and financial partners depend on its place at the center of the booming AI economy. “I’m responsible for an entire industry,” Huang recently told Bloomberg in a sit-down.

A spokesperson for the Chinese Embassy in Washington dismissed the allegations in late October as “groundless smears and slanders,” and no one has been charged. But U.S. intelligence officials reportedly briefed President Donald Trump about their concerns a year ago, and unnamed senior officials inside the Trump administration told the Times that they doubt Huang could have remained unaware all that time of Alice Huang’s intelligence-gathering role for China. When Trump was first briefed, the Times reported, officials didn’t believe Huang knew, and isn’t clear whether their assessment has changed. The board, meanwhile, says it sees no sign that national security or company secrets were compromised, and that Huang is staying on.

The question now is whether Huang can survive this. To think through what could come next, this editor spoke Sunday with famed professor Jeffrey Sonnenfeld, senior associate dean for leadership studies at the Yale School of Management and founder of the Chief Executive Leadership Institute, which has hosted Huang at its CEO summits. Sonnenfeld likes Huang, he said, and he called Huang’s career salvageable but put the odds at “about a 50-50.” He also had a lot more food for thought.

First, the reputational damage. Until now, Huang has been “revered and trusted by everybody in technology,” Sonnenfeld noted. But he called this episode “remarkably poor judgment.” Even if Huang was merely naive, he said, “any employee in Nvidia other than him would be fired over this,” and the recklessness alone would mean “an irretrievable loss of confidence.”

Sonnenfeld suggested that Huang’s saving grace could be what social psychologists call idiosyncrasy credits, if he can prove he was being duped. The term comes from Edwin Hollander, a social psychologist who proposed in a 1958 paper that people earn latitude in a group by showing competence and following its rules. Huang has been earning latitude since he co-founded Nvidia in 1993. But all that goodwill can run out when a screw-up looks self-serving or touches the group’s core norms. Here that group includes Nvidia’s board, its roughly 42,000 employees who are bound by security rules, and, in a looser sense, the U.S. government, given it restricts its exports.

We also talked about precedents, except, as it turns out, there really are none. Executives who survive personal scandals usually pick up where they left off at another company, Sonnenfeld observed. Former HP CEO Mark Hurd is one example example. Between 2005 and 2010, he turned HP to the top of the industry by revenue, then was pressured to resign after he filed inaccurate expense reports that hid a personal relationship with a contractor. Oracle hired him about a month later. Huang has no equivalent move to make because Nvidia is his company

As for the bench at Nvidia, that doesn’t really exist either. While there is typically a hierarchy inside companies of Nvidia’s scale and importance, Huang has kept things as flat as possible, with about 60 direct reports by his own account. That structure makes it hard to figure out who would succeed him from inside (though his children, both of whom work at Nvidia, are the sometimes the subject of succession speculation). That won’t stop the worldwide guessing game that’s sure to follow in the coming weeks. And boards in crisis often reach for a “messianic solution” from outside, Sonnenfeld said, floating former Google CEO Eric Schmidt as one idea.

Nvidia’s role at the center of a massive and complex circular financing web is what makes this particular predicament especially tricky. Nvidia isn’t just the biggest provider of chips driving the AI boom but also largely the bank behind the buildout. In addition to stakes in hundreds of smaller startups, Nvidia has invested in OpenAI, Anthropic, xAI, CoreWeave and Nebius. In August, it also signed preliminary agreements with Apollo, BlackRock, Blackstone, Brookfield, Goldman Sachs and KKR aimed at mobilizing more than $500 billion over time, and Huang has said Nvidia could backstop up to a quarter of it. The move was widely interpreted as a way to spread risk. Still, a leadership crisis at the center is how a stumble becomes a spiral.

So what’s Nvidia’s board likely doing right now? Sonnenfeld noted that Huang’s advocacy for selling advanced chips to China had already made people anxious. Anthropic’s Dario Amodei, whose company Nvidia has backed, had a lot to say about selling chips to China at Davos back in January, likening it to “selling nuclear weapons to North Korea.”

Now the board’s independent members — meaning everyone but Huang — will have to establish what technology reached China if any, and by what routes, including by better understanding Nvidia’s relationship with Megaspeed. The Singapore cloud company, which grew out of a Chinese gaming business and became Nvidia’s largest buyer in Southeast Asia, bought roughly $2 billion of its chips, the New York Times reported in October 2025, raising broader questions about whether it was helping China circumvent U.S. export restrictions, either by re-exporting the chips to China or renting them to Chinese customers remotely. (An Nvidia spokesperson told The Hill after the Times report that the allegations were “thoroughly investigated and debunked last year.”)

In the meantime, Huang will keep running the company, Nvidia said Sunday. That’ll be a tall order, even for a CEO who already spends much of his time traveling the world to promote Nvidia. For starters, three House chairmen want a closed-door briefing from Huang on November 18. Nvidia is also reporting its newest earnings on November 17. Sonnenfeld expects that even if Huang keeps the title, he’ll have to hand off some of the day-to-day decision-making.

Only one thing is certain right now. Huang doesn’t own the kind of super-voting stock that would cement his power at Nvidia. Sonnenfeld often argues for dual-class structures that empower founders, and he says Huang’s success without one has been the best counterargument to that case. But if Huang had one, Sonnenfeld continued, removing him would be harder.

This episode may be remembered as a pivotal moment for how the tech industry guards its secrets. Sonnenfeld argues tech has been “way too lax” compared with defense contractors that face clearance regimes. The arrest last month of an OpenAI engineer points to the same problem. He was accused of hiding a recording device behind a TV in an executive conference room, and while OpenAI has said it has no indication that model weights, source code, or customer data were accessed, the case highlights how exposed tech companies are to people who get close.

Huang may well survive the investigation (Sonnenfeld hopes the board clears him of knowingly aiding anyone). The bigger test comes when Nvidia’s employees, customers and partners, and, indirectly, the millions of Americans with Nvidia in their 401(k) plans, have to decide whether an AI economy built largely on the judgment of Huang still makes sense.

A natural varnish was discovered on 37,000-year-old horse paintings in Chauvet Cave using ultraviolet light

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

A UV probe used from up to 8 feet (2.5 meters) away found a translucent calcite layer over the 37,000-year-old charcoal horses of Chauvet Cave.

In Chauvet Cave, in the Ardèche gorge of southern France, researchers set up a tripod on the steel walkway in the Hillaire Chamber and ran a carbon fiber pole out toward the Horses Panel.

Artists drew those horses in charcoal about 37,000 years ago. A lens at the pole’s tip sat 2 inches (5 centimeters) off the rock and lit it with ultraviolet light. The operator, meanwhile, stayed up to 8 feet (2.5 meters) back on the walkway.

What came back showed a translucent skin of calcite over parts of the black lines. The team called it a natural varnish and plotted the spots where it shows on the panel. Still, the survey cannot say how thick it is.

Jean-Marie Chauvet, Éliette Brunel and Christian Hillaire got into the cave on Sunday, December 18, 1994, according to the French Ministry of Culture, and on Christmas Eve they went back and put a plastic strip down over the path they had used.

The steel walkways follow that strip today. Per the ministry’s page on the cave layout, they are 2 feet (60 centimeters) wide. In the main chambers, they run for 1,214 feet (370 meters). Crews also widened the old entrance and fitted it with a heavy reinforced door.

There are animal tracks and human footprints on the floor, so everyone works from the walkways only. The paper puts the walls 3 to 20 feet (1 to 6 meters) away from them. At the panels in this study, the gap is 5 to 10 feet (1.5 to 3 meters).

At that range the team ruled out X-ray fluorescence and Raman spectroscopy, the standard pigment tools. Neither can tell bedrock limestone from calcite left later by dripping water anyway, as both are calcite.

First author Yohan Boulard works at LAMS, the Laboratory of Molecular and Structural Archaeology of Sorbonne University and the CNRS in Paris. He designed the instrument with Thiéry Guillou and Philippe Walter, the corresponding author.

Light came from a 1.3-watt OSRAM LED at 365 nanometers. It went up the pole through one of seven fibers in a 10-foot (3-meter) bundle, and the other six carried the glow back to an Ocean Optics USB4000 spectrometer, with a 24-volt battery for power and a Python program on a laptop to log each reading.

Dyed papers left under the beam did not fade. So the team took the light as safe for the walls. Humidity in the chamber ran at about 100 percent, at 55 degrees Fahrenheit (13 degrees Celsius).

In the lab, calcite sampled from Chauvet for earlier work glowed with one blue peak, near 450 nanometers. However, limestone from near the site showed that peak and another near 580.

When the team dissolved the calcite in hydrochloric acid, the blue stayed in the liquid. That tied it to organic matter from the surface ecosystem.

Carole Fritz, of LAMS and the CREAP center in Toulouse, led the cave’s scientific team. She also supervised the study with Walter and Maguy Jaber. The group took 60 spectra in all, 47 of them on the Horses Panel.

Over the black lines the signal dropped to as little as one-twentieth of what bare rock gave. Charcoal soaks up ultraviolet light without glowing, so any light at all off a line meant something was lying over it.

The lip of the central horse gave almost only the blue calcite peak. Bare limestone beside it gave both peaks, and the jaw, where the charcoal covers the rock only in part, fell in between.

The authors pointed out that thick crusts can hide paintings but a clear one can “behave like a natural varnish.” For example, at Arcy-sur-Cure’s Grande Grotte in the Yonne department, an opaque calcite crust hid the Paleolithic paintings. They stayed hidden until restorers rubbed it off.

Uranium-thorium dates on such crusts were used to claim that Neanderthals made cave art in Spain more than 64,000 years ago, and a long list of rock art specialists rejected that reading in a 2020 paper in the Journal of Human Evolution.

The Alcove of the Lions, in the same sector as the Horses Panel, gave a different picture. Water got in there after people left the cave, and it left flowstone, calcite laid down in sheets where water runs over rock.

The team recorded eleven spectra in the alcove. On the left, flowstone pours out of a horizontal crack that crosses a horse’s head. Over that head, the blue glow was bright enough to show in the photographs.

Farther down, on a horse’s breast, the calcite grew on bare rock and stopped at the charcoal lines. Those lines gave no calcite signal at all.

The team took this as a sign that the charcoal affected how the crystals formed. Earlier work on iron and manganese oxide pigments, by contrast, found no such link.

Seeping water also coated the Signs Panel in Chauvet’s Red Panel Chamber, according to the ministry, and the same mineral builds the stalagmites that rise from cave floors.

Some charcoal marks had orange patches just under them in the ultraviolet photographs, and the spectra from those spots peaked near 580 nanometers.

The team burned Scots pine at 752, 932 and 1,292 degrees Fahrenheit (400, 500 and 700 degrees Celsius) to make test charcoal, because the cave’s own charcoal came from that tree, as an earlier study in Antiquity found. Its tar glowed blue.

The team also heated limestone and calcite samples to 932 degrees Fahrenheit (500 degrees Celsius) for one hour or 24 hours. Afterward, they glowed orange. The same orange showed up near torch smears, the marks people left on the wall when they revived a torch.

The authors suggested that some lines were drawn with charcoal that had “hot, possibly burning tips,” and they wrote that this is not direct proof.

At Font-de-Gaume, another French cave, charcoal from black drawings has been used for dating, to between 13,000 and 16,000 years ago.

There is no thickness for the varnish in the paper, in millimeters or anything else. Nobody made calcite layers of known thickness to calibrate against. The authors also wrote that grown layers like the cave’s would be very hard to copy.

The blue signal depends on both the thickness of the calcite and how much organic matter it holds. In the Alcove of the Lions, flowstone and thin veils formed in different ways. So nobody can compare the numbers there one to one.

The burning-charcoal idea still needs testing. For example, studies could look at how far the charcoal burned. They could also test how heat changes clays with more or less iron oxide. Also, the survey covered only one sector of the cave, the Horses Sector.

The authors called the method “a promising tool” for deciding how decorated caves are protected and who gets in. For now, the readings taken from the Hillaire Chamber walkway show a translucent calcite layer over the horses.

The full study was published in the journal ACS Omega.

Oracle Stock Is on Pace for Its Worst Year Since 2001. Here's What Followed Its Only 2 Worse Years.

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The year after one of them, shares climbed 84%. The year after the other, they kept sliding.

Oracle (ORCL +0.82%) is having a terrible 2026. As of this writing, shares trade near $113, about 42% under the $194.91 they closed at last year, and they recently touched a new 52-week low.

Going by daily closing prices (price only, adjusted for stock splits), that puts the stock on pace for its worst calendar year since 2001. Only two full years since Oracle's March 1986 IPO were worse: 1990, the worst of them, when shares dropped 66%, and 2001, when they fell 52%. No other year cost shareholders over 22%.

What came next went in opposite directions. Shares soared 84% in 1991. In 2002, they dropped another 22%.

Granted, two cases make a thin record, and I wouldn't read either as a forecast. But what separated those two years deserves a closer look.

Oracle's 1990 troubles were mostly its own. Its revenue forecasts turned out too rosy, and in the quarter ended Aug. 31, 1990, it reported its first-ever quarterly loss. The company shed around 400 jobs, or about 10% of its U.S. staff, and its chief financial officer stepped down.

Before adjusting for later stock splits, shares slid from $28.125 in March 1990 to $6.875 on Sept. 17.

Still, the business itself kept growing. Oracle's revenue climbed around 12% to $1.03 billion in fiscal 1991 (the year ended May 31, 1991), even as operating income dropped from $135 million to $18 million and the company reported a net loss of $12.4 million. A year later, revenue grew 15% and net income rebounded to $61.5 million.

And by the end of 1990, the stock was cheap. The whole company was valued at around one year's worth of the revenue it would book in fiscal 1991.

The rebound didn't begin right away. By mid-January 1991, shares traded around 29% below where they ended 1990. Then the stock surged, closing the year up 84%. It had help -- the Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC +1.09%) climbed 57% in 1991.

The 2001 fall was different. The dot-com crash hurt the companies that bought Oracle's software, and the Nasdaq Composite dropped 21% that year.

This time, demand gave way. Oracle's revenue fell about 12% to $9.7 billion in fiscal 2002 (the year ended May 31, 2002), then dipped again in fiscal 2003. And even after halving, the stock ended 2001 at about eight times fiscal 2002 revenue and about 35 times fiscal 2002 earnings.

Shares rallied early in 2002, then resumed falling. By June 2002, they were over 40% below their 2001 close, and they finished every trading day from mid-March 2002 through the end of 2003 under that mark.

On the two things that separated those years, 2026 arguably looks more like 1990.

First, this drop is Oracle's own. The S&P 500 (^GSPC +0.31%) has gained around 8% so far in 2026, as I write.

What's more, demand is accelerating. Oracle's revenue climbed 17% in fiscal 2026 (the year ended May 31, 2026) and 21% in its fourth quarter. Growth then reached 30% in the fiscal first quarter of 2027 (the period ended Aug. 31, 2026), when revenue hit $19.3 billion. Management expects at least $90 billion in revenue this fiscal year, or growth of around 34%.

The trouble is paying for it. Oracle's capital spending topped the cash its operations took in by $23.7 billion in fiscal 2026, and by around $5 billion more in fiscal Q1 alone.

On Nov. 2, it priced $10 billion of new stock at $116 a share alongside $10 billion of notes with interest rates of 7% to 8.65%. Even the shortest of those notes, due in 2029, yields around 2.4 percentage points more than a comparable Treasury.

On price, Oracle falls between the two earlier years. Shares sell for around four times the revenue management expects this fiscal year, versus about one times sales in late 1990 and about eight times sales in late 2001.

But two cases can't say much about 2027, and 1991's rebound came with a tech rally that nobody could count on today. Oracle's cash might matter more. Management expects free cash flow to turn positive in the second half of fiscal 2028.

I've been leery of Oracle stock for most of this year, mainly because of the cash it's spending. But with the stock around 14 times this fiscal year's expected non-GAAP (adjusted) earnings of $8.10 a share, I think a small stake is worth considering. I'd keep it small till the cash starts coming back.

Daniel Sparks and his clients do not have positions in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Oracle. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Robert De Niro Screens ‘Musk’ Documentary, Needles Elon About Spending Spree On GOP: “How Did That Turn Out For You?”

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Elon Musk isn’t having much of a week. His spent hundreds of millions of dollars on the midterm elections, only to have his preferred Republican candidates lose. And now he’s taking it on the chin from Robert De Niro.

The Oscar-winning actor was joined by his fellow Tribeca Festival founder Jane Rosenthal for a screening in New York City Sunday of Musk, Alex Gibney’s documentary that is highly critical of the world’s richest man. We understand a roomful of Academy members was on hand for the event at the Crosby Hotel in Manhattan.

“Elon Musk spent about 300 million dollars getting Trump elected in 2024,” De Niro said as he introduced the film. “And he just spent close to half a billion on Republicans in this past week’s election. How did that turn out for you?” he said to cheers and laughter. Related Stories News 'SNL' Makes Grok Do "Epic Vulgar Roast" Of Elon Musk On 'Weekend Update': "The Worst Thing South Africa Has Ever Done" News Deadline's Sound & Screen Film Underway To Start The Awards-Season Drumbeat

De Niro alluded to more bad news for Musk this week – his announcement on X that Tesla would end production of the much-ballyhooed Cybertruck next summer. Watch on Deadline

“In the fight between Musk’s money and democracy, democracy won. This time. But just like his grotesque Cybertruck, Musk isn’t going anywhere,” De Niro commented. “It’s a reminder that while we are celebrating the massive electoral repudiation of Trump and Trumpism, we have to be vigilant — and not just vigilant, we have to be ready to take action. This is an important film, I hope you will be as moved as I was by it and spread the word.”

For his part, Oscar winner Gibney told De Niro, “Thank you, Bob, for all you have done for all of the resistance.”

Musk, with a running time of 3 hours 52 minutes, is now available on VOD platforms. Released theatrically by Bleecker Street, the documentary has made over $700,000 at the domestic box office.

“I admit I was apprehensive before watching this film, the first time I saw it. I mean four hours, Alex, really?” Rosenthal kidded Gibney. “Four hours in the company of Elon Musk — it is a lot to ask. It is a thoroughly engrossing film about a thoroughly gross character. A character we cannot ignore.”

Rosenthal added, “2,500 years ago, Sun Tzu [author of The Art of War] cautioned us to know your enemy. Musk the film exposes Musk the man and we cannot look away.”

On Sunday night, Musk won Best Biographical Documentary at the Critics Choice Documentary Awards in New York. It premiered at the Venice Film Festival and went on to screen at the Toronto Film Festival, Camden International Film Festival, Zurich Film Festival, BFI London Film Festival and more. It screens later this week at DOC NYC after making that festival’s prestigious shortlist of the year’s best documentaries. Musk is also slated to screen this month at IDFA in Amsterdam, the world’s biggest documentary film festival.

The 6 Best DC Movie Opening Shots, Ranked

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Feel free to connect with him or check out his work. He's everywhere — Upwork, YouTube, Spotify, SoundCloud, Collider, LinkedIn, Instagram.

Opening shots are absolutely crucial in movies. They’re meant to hook you into the story instantly and establish the core rules and tone of everything that follows. And if the filmmakers get it right, they know the audience is theirs for the next two hours. That’s exactly why an opening is usually one of the best sequences in any movie.

And a good opening is even more important for the superhero genre, which is built on scale and spectacle. The genre has consistently delivered banger openings year after year, and DC has had more than its fair share. Today, we’re sifting through the DC movies and bringing you some of the greatest openings they’ve ever delivered.

A lot of people praise the opening sequence where Batman (Robert Pattinson) reflects on his second year as a vigilante in a rain-soaked, crime-ridden Gotham City on Halloween night. But that’s not how the movie opens. It actually opens with a claustrophobic POV of a masked figure, breathing heavily, stalking a family through binoculars. At first, you think it’s Batman scouting a criminal. Then a young boy comes out dressed in a red ninja costume, practicing martial arts moves with a toy sword, and you go, oh no, is that Bruce Wayne? Are they doing his parents’ murder again? Then you realize it’s none of those things.

It was actually the Riddler’s POV (Paul Dano), and you’d been watching him stalk his first victim, the Mayor of Gotham. These subtle misdirections, one after another, were so damn good. Then the Riddler steps out of the shadows, murders the Mayor in cold blood, and wraps his face in tape. This wasn’t the huge spectacle opening we’ve come to expect from the superhero genre. But it perfectly established the film’s slow-burn psychological horror tone and painted Gotham as a city drowning in crime and corruption, where not even the Mayor is safe in his own home.

Watchmen opens in an alternate-history 1985 with the murder of an aging vigilante called the Comedian (Jeffrey Dean Morgan). It’s filmed in Zack Snyder’s hallmark slow-motion style and meticulously choreographed to Nat King Cole’s “Unforgettable.” The Comedian is alone in his high-rise New York apartment when a mysterious, super-strong intruder breaks in, and the two erupt into a fierce hand-to-hand fight.

The Comedian is absolutely overpowered. The apartment gets destroyed. And blood flies through the air and splatters perfectly across a yellow smiley-face pin resting on his counter, recreating the iconic imagery from the comics. The sequence ends with the attacker throwing Blake through a glass window and sending him plummeting to the pavement below, which sets up the murder mystery that drives the entire movie.

Superman opens in the most comic book way it possibly could. We’re in the Antarctic snow, and a timeline of on-screen text walks you through the new DCU and Superman’s (David Corenswet) story so far. Three centuries ago, the first metahumans appeared. Three decades ago, Clark arrived on Earth. Three years ago, he revealed himself to the world as Superman. Three hours ago, the Hammer of Boravia attacked. And three minutes ago, Superman lost a battle for the first time.

It establishes the stakes so much better than any origin montage or fight sequence ever could. Usually, you’d establish the status quo, and then move to the conflict where Superman loses his first fight. But James Gunn straight up opens the movie with its aftermath! Immediately after, Superman crash-lands in the snow, bloody, whistling for Krypto while the Superman theme swells up behind him, and it instantly hypes you up for the rest of the movie.

Four of the largest communities on the planet, and four completely different reasons to belong to one. Here is yours, what it would give you, and the argument you would be having within a week.

You belong to the fandom that is really about comfort. Hogwarts works on people because it is a place rather than a plot, and the pull is not the wizard war so much as the corridors, the food, the weather and the sense that somebody is expecting you.

You belong to the deepest fandom on this list, the one where somebody has always read further than you and is quietly delighted to say so. Middle-earth rewards patience more than any other world in popular culture.

You belong to the loudest and largest tent of the four, the one where three generations are all watching the same thing and none of them agree about any of it. That disagreement is not a flaw in this fandom, it is the main activity.

You belong to the thinking fandom, the one that turns up for the argument rather than the explosion. Trek has always been a series of ethical problems in space, and the people who love it love being asked what they would have done.

The Dark Knight Rises opens at thousands of feet in the air with Bane (Tom Hardy) sitting in a CIA plane, hooded and chained. But you quickly realize he’s not a prisoner. He let himself get caught on purpose, because he needed to extract a high-value target who also happened to be on this plane. The CIA agent asks what the next step of his master plan is, and Bane looks up and calmly says, “Crashing this plane... with no survivors!” Suddenly, a massive transport plane drops out of the sky above them. Four mercenaries rappel down and rip the CIA plane in half. And they just hold it there, nose down, hanging from their own plane.

Shootouts and fistfights break out inside the cabin, and Bane manages to kill them all. And then, he even goes so far as to leave one of his own men behind so the body count matches the flight manifest. Bane escapes with his target and the rest of his team out the back of the fuselage as the plane breaks apart and crashes into the mountains. Christopher Nolan used real aircraft and practical stunt work, so it obviously looks insanely good. But it also establishes Bane as someone Batman (Christian Bale) has never really faced before, a villain who’s just as smart as he is, and a lot stronger.

The movie’s very first shot starts on a puddle’s reflection in a prison yard and tilts up to show Savant (Michael Rooker). He’s bouncing a rubber ball off the walls and floor with pinpoint accuracy, hitting marked “X” targets, and the ball lands right back in his hand. A small yellow canary flies into his cell, and Savant purposely bounces the ball around the room one last time, striking and killing the bird.

From there, Amanda Waller (Viola Davis) recruits him, and the movie transitions to the hilarious plane sequence where the squad of misfits meets each other for the first time. And it leads to the iconic bait-and-switch in Corto Maltese, where almost every character is wiped out in the first 10 minutes. It was completely unexpected, and it quickly established that this wasn’t going to be your typical superhero story and that no one was safe.

The Dark Knight begins with one of the best heist sequences ever filmed. The camera zooms into a glass skyscraper, a window shatters from the inside, and two men in clown masks use a zip-line to launch themselves across to the roof of Gotham National Bank. Meanwhile, a van screeches up to a street corner, where a man holding a clown mask is waiting. He hops in, and the men inside start talking about the man who planned the heist, the one they call Joker (Heath Ledger).

The crew storms the bank and begins the heist, but they also start methodically taking each other out, just like they’ve been ordered. Each one thinks he’s removing the weak link so there’s a bigger share for everyone else. None of them knows someone else is ready to take them out too. It continues until there’s just one of them left, and the getaway driver arrives in a school bus and shoots the last guy, revealing himself to be the Joker. He places a smoke grenade tied to a pin in the bank manager’s mouth, steps onto the school bus filled with millions of dollars in mob money, and pulls out into a line of identical school buses as the police sirens approach. It’s widely considered one of the greatest openings of all time across any genre.

Scientists poured dye into a parking lot drain to prove that toxic runoff was quickly polluting the fragile ecosystem of Wind Cave

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Dye tracing at South Dakota's Wind Cave revealed how surface water can reach underground lakes and aquifers within hours, exposing pollution risks.

In 1996, National Park Service staff poured a dye called rhodamine WT into a parking lot drain at Wind Cave National Park, in South Dakota. Within hours, dye levels rose at monitoring points in the cave below.

In a later test, a dye called fluorescein briefly turned Calcite Lake, a lake deep inside the cave, bright green.

These dye traces show how fast surface water, and anything it carries, can reach the cave and the Madison Aquifer beneath it.

Wind Cave became the eighth U.S. national park in 1903 and the first created to protect a cave, according to the U.S. Geological Survey (USGS).

Groundwater drips from the cave ceiling in many places. It collects in ponds, lakes and streams on the cave floor.

The cave lies in the Madison Formation, a layer of limestone and dolomite about 330 feet (100 meters) thick. Most locations above it are capped by the base of the Minnelusa Formation, which includes sandstone, limestone and shale.

Below the park, the Madison Aquifer supplies water to the park and to parts of South Dakota and Wyoming.

Park facilities sit on thin ground over the cave, which raised concern among park staff.

Dye tracing at the park began in 1986, when graduate student Marsha Davis used fluorescent dye tracing near park headquarters. In mid-July, she injected fluorescein into the ground.

Later that summer, Davis injected rhodamine into the same parking lot drain to simulate a rain event.

Rhodamine turned up at a few cave sites a couple of days later. The fluorescein detection was likely a false positive, and a rhodamine signal in the park well probably came from a separate trace at Beaver Creek.

The study produced no clear results. Lacking records of wet cave areas, Davis relied on cavers’ memories to choose sampling sites.

In mid-June 1993, James Nepstad of the National Park Service injected rhodamine over the cave’s Club Room. A month later, he added fluorescein above the cave’s southeast section.

Two years later, neither dye had appeared in cave samples. Those sites lay farther from park facilities, under thicker ground, which pointed to slow or sideways flow.

The 1996 drain test gave the clearest signal. Dye levels climbed fast, began falling almost two weeks later and rose again after later rain.

Fluorescein injected at a picnic area turned up at the spot of Davis’s earlier false positive, about 1,470 feet (449 meters) away. Other northern sites showed no dye over the following year.

The researchers concluded that water moves across the land first and then down into the cave.

In a 2015 conference paper, Nepstad reported that a variety of cave sites with dripping or pooled water were monitored for up to five years after dye injection.

“Transit times to the cave varied from less than six hours to as much as 4.8 years,” he wrote. Travel time showed little link to how far the dye had to go, either sideways or down.

Spills of chemicals or sewage near the injection sites would quickly enter several underground sites and could persist for years, the paper warns.

In 2008, Marc Ohms, a physical science technician at the park, injected fluorescein into What the Hell Lake. He then monitored Rebel River and Calcite Lake, two other water bodies in the cave.

A month after injection, dye was visible in all three. Laboratory analysis confirmed fluorescein at Rebel River and Calcite Lake, showing that water flows from What the Hell Lake to both.

Calcite Lake glowed bright green for a short time. The park wells showed no dye.

Another trace began at a hole in the bed of Highland Creek that swallowed water in 2008. Lab tests detected no rhodamine at Calcite Lake or in the park wells.

Park researchers concluded that the creek water does not reach the cave or the wells.

The Madison Aquifer’s water table is reached at the deepest part of the cave, where the underground lakes lie. These lakes connect to the regional aquifer, which extends into North Dakota, Montana, Saskatchewan and Manitoba.

The area known as The Lakes sits 490 feet (149 meters) below the surface, according to a 2004 park announcement. Herb Conn and David Schnute discovered the first lake in 1968.

“This is the only place in South Dakota where it is possible to see the water table,” Ohms said in 2004.

The lake water is extremely pure, with very little biological material, a 2010 survey by microbiologist Dr. Hazel Barton found. A December 2010 sample identified about 350 bacterial species, many not known before.

In 2002 and 2003, a USGS analysis found chemicals from consumer goods and septic systems in the park’s three streams.

Polluted water could harm the cave ecosystem and make water from the aquifer unsafe to drink, a risk also flagged for cave groundwater in Albania.

The information was obtained from the National Park Service.

John Oliver Weighs In on Midterm Elections, Elon Musk and the Ultimate Southern Diss

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

“Democrats won so big on Tuesday it was sometimes genuinely hard to take all the positive results in," the 'Last Week Tonight' hosted said on Sunday's show.

John Oliver kicked off Sunday’s Last Week Tonight by running down a list of several Democratic wins from last week’s midterm elections.

The show started out with a blue wave crashing through the intro before Oliver cheerfully recapped the news: “It has been a busy week and, given the midterm results, a pretty good week too. Democrats won so big on Tuesday it was sometimes genuinely hard to take all the positive results in.”

He listed several big Democratic victories — earning loud applause from the audience after each — including the historically red Lone Star State: “It was genuinely hard to believe Texas had just turned blue.” Related Stories News Trump Says He Was Just Kidding About Making UFC's Dana White His Press Secretary General News Trump Reflects on Republicans' Midterm Losses, Reacts to J.D. Vance's Own Analysis in '60 Minutes' Interview

Oliver then turned his attention to a certain billionaire who spent a lot of money backing candidates who were ultimately defeated. “Then it hits you, Elon Musk wasted so much money,” he said. “The half a billion dollars he spent to try and buy the election just went up in flames faster than a parked Cybertruck. Speaking of which, we also learned this week he’s ending production of Cybertrucks, which isn’t technically an election result but kind of feels like one.”

Oliver also spotlighted the Mississippi Senate race between Scott Colom and Cindy Hyde-Smith. It’s still too close to call, but the Last Week Tonight host noted that Hyde-Smith “appeared to have prematurely declared victory” on Wednesdsay. Colom, he said, “had a quintessentially Southern response.”

Oliver showed a clip of Colom speaking to reporters. When asked about Hyde-Smith’s apparent gaffe, Colom paused for several seconds before saying, “Bless her heart.”

Explained Oliver: “If you are not from the South, you should know it is basically not possible to say anything meaner than that down there. ‘Bless your heart’ was essentially invented in the South as a way to commit legal murder on someone without getting out of your church pew.”

The NYT Opens Pandora’s Letterboxd; Roku Joins The Skulk

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The New York Times is in exclusive talks to acquire Letterboxd, the film fan and review site, valuing the publisher at about $330 million. That’s per a recent report from The New York Times, although rumors have swirled all year of a Letterboxd acquisition.

The deal would put Letterboxd’s advertising under the NYT, which should improve its earnings. Letterboxd is reportedly on pace to earn only $15 million this year. It’s not a huge business, but the Times has deliberately pushed into new verticals, often through acquisition: product reviews (Wirecutter), cooking, games (Wordle) and sports (The Athletic).

But one thing that’s often missed in Letterboxd deal discussions – in particular, why it’s sought a high valuation (20x annual earnings) at an absolute nadir for web publisher earnings – is its AI data licensing value.

Letterboxd is an authenticated forum with a massive library of unique human writing that is rich with banter, recommendations, drama, culture, etc. It is, in other words, a rich repository for LLMs.

The New York Times will be more effective at monetizing that asset as well.

Surprise! Fox Corporation announced the completion of its $22 billion Roku acquisition on Friday.

The timing might be unexpected, but the result is not, given that CEO Lachlan Murdoch told investors during last Monday’s earnings call that he expected the deal to close “as soon as possible.” Semafor also reported that the Department of Justice (DOJ) was about to clear the deal under a consent decree, even though it had just recently asked for additional information from both companies in September.

Fox declined to comment to AdExchanger about the deal, but the DOJ’s Antitrust Division released a statement on Friday, saying that the merged company “would suffer monetary and reputational harm if it attempted to use the Roku platform to harm Fox’s content rivals,” and that consumers could easily switch to other connected TV platforms if they were dissatisfied with the product.

Regardless of what happened behind the scenes, Fox now officially owns the top two most popular free streaming services in the US: The Roku Channel (a FAST service) and Tubi (an AVOD streamer). It also has access to all of Roku’s ad tech capabilities and first-party data, allowing the company to massively scale up its connected TV advertising business.

In particular, being able to integrate Roku’s household graph with Tubi’s ad sales would be a real benefit to Fox, which is already seeing more success than it expected from the latter business.

Apple takes the cake with surprise rug-pulling changes. But Google’s no slouch.

For instance, the past few changes have been tough on open web video inventory. CPMs, fill rates and proxies for demand have dropped since about October 26, as Alan Ronis writes at Medium. The Rube Goldberg cascade of events started on October 1, when DV360 removed brand safety controls and thus some advertisers who relied on its finer-grain controls. On October 22, AdSense began restricting more web video inventory based on the accompanying content. And the big whammy was October 26, when Google retired the video line item for mCPV (maximum cost-per-view) campaigns.

The deadline was communicated, but publishers and ad tech didn’t upgrade, and many campaigns that had been preset just failed to serve.

Prebid, by the way, had a similar problem this year because publishers simply couldn’t be made to update their video ad delivery setup when Microsoft reached its deadline for paying to cache all Prebid video ads.

OpenAI is building a new tool that will show brands how often they’re mentioned in ChatGPT conversations. [post]

Online publication consortium Valnet agrees to a $2 million settlement in a class action lawsuit alleging the company of misclassifying and exploiting its freelance writers. [TheWrap]

Meta won’t require business account holders to allow AI training on its linked websites after all. [Ars Technica]

Instagram’s new push into long-form content hasn’t quite convinced creators to get on board yet. [Digiday]

Liam Parker is appointed US CEO of The Ogilvy Group. [release]

Why the midterms may not spell doom for ad tech consolidation. Plus, how sports leagues are putting ad revenue before player safety.

Agentic media buying is the No. 1 topic du jour in ad tech discussion circles. But how does agentic buying even work when it comes to buying TV and streaming ads? And how does that workflow differ from programmatic? The startup Swivel aims to address those questions.

AppLovin is growing at a blistering pace, but Wall Street investors remain cautious about the business.

High-end ad blockers mimic some of the same behavior as bot traffic. So WiseGuyGaming repurposed the tools it was already using to identify bots to reveal its ad-blocking audience as well.

Criteo has weathered considerable storms in its day, including a painful moment in March 2020 when its market cap bottomed out below $500 million. Can it pull off yet another turnaround?

Disaffection and outright anger toward US sports institutions run deeper than logo-cluttered fields. Leagues and teams are sacrificing player safety and fan enjoyment for marketing dollars.
