Circle’s USDC Transaction Volume Rises 49% Even as Reserve Income Faces Pressure

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: Circle reported $13.9 trillion of USDC onchain transaction volume in the third quarter of 2026, up 49% year over year, while average USDC circulation reached $73.3 billion. The issuer’s results show strong network activity, but that does not translate directly into equivalent revenue growth.

Stablecoin businesses are often judged by a single number: how many dollars’ worth of tokens are circulating. Circle’s latest earnings suggest that approach misses a substantial part of what is happening around USDC, and also oversimplifies how the company makes money.

In its November 4 SEC-filed results, Circle reported $13.9 trillion in USDC onchain transaction volume for the quarter, an increase of 49% from a year earlier. Average USDC in circulation rose 8% to $73.3 billion, while total revenue and reserve income increased 17% to $865 million.

A stablecoin can move between users many times without the amount outstanding increasing. That makes onchain transfer volume a useful measure of network activity but a poor substitute for payment revenue, spending by real-world customers or income retained by the issuer.

Some onchain activity also reflects exchange settlement, liquidity management and transfers between controlled addresses. It would be a mistake to present $13.9 trillion as a tally of retail purchases or to assume the growth rate proves stablecoins have replaced ordinary banking payments.

Circle’s net income from continuing operations reached $226 million, up 5% year on year. Adjusted EBITDA increased 85% to $317 million. Differences in the growth rates reflect a changing mix of operational, distribution and accounting factors; investors should assess the underlying disclosures rather than treating all three numbers as interchangeable signals.

Circle highlighted that its payments network reached $41.3 billion in annualised total payment volume during the quarter. It also announced plans to acquire Tazapay, a cross-border payments platform, with completion expected in 2027 subject to approvals. These initiatives aim to make it easier for businesses to move between traditional payment systems and digital-dollar settlement.

This strategy faces a practical challenge: issuing a token is relatively simple compared with connecting a broad set of banking, compliance and payout arrangements. Customers require dependable redemption, useful local currency access and clear rules. Onchain transaction volumes alone do not tell us whether those services are profitable.

The competitive environment is also changing. As NewsBTC noted when some stablecoins faced new European trading restrictions, distribution is shaped by regulation as well as technology. An issuer needs both.

Beyond the headline USDC supply, the key numbers are revenue after distribution costs, payment volumes generated by identifiable services, changes in the reserve-income environment and the cost of expanding infrastructure. Circle has shown activity and profit growth, but it must continue converting that network usage into durable economics.

The bigger story is a maturing business model. Stablecoins increasingly compete as financial infrastructure, not simply as exchange trading pairs. Circle’s quarter offers evidence that USDC is moving at scale, while also reminding readers that volume, adoption and shareholder returns are three different questions.

Original Medicare Has No Out-of-Pocket Limit – Add One Before You Need Care

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Original Medicare puts no yearly limit on your out-of-pocket costs. Cap them with a Medigap policy like Plan G or a Medicare Advantage plan. If you choose Medigap, buy it in your six-month open enrollment window, when insurers can’t turn you down or charge more for your health.

Millions go without either one. KFF found that in 2023, 3.5 million people, 13% of everyone in traditional Medicare, lacked any supplemental coverage.

Without it, your share of Part B care is usually 20% of the Medicare-approved amount after the $283 deductible. By our math, a $100,000 year of outpatient cancer treatment leaves you more than $20,000 to pay. Part A adds a $1,736 hospital deductible in 2026 that you can owe more than once a year, plus daily charges for long hospital and skilled nursing stays.

Your Medigap window opens the first month you’re 65 or older with Part B, and it never repeats. Outside it, insurers in most states can ask health questions, then turn you down or charge more, unless a guaranteed issue right applies, such as your Advantage plan pulling out of your area.

To price Medigap, start with Mutual of Omaha. AM Best rates Mutual of Omaha Insurance Company A+ (Superior). Its online tool quotes Plan G and High-Deductible Plan G from your ZIP code, birth date, and sex, without asking for contact details. On Sept. 23, 2026, it quoted High-Deductible Plan G at $45.84 to $52.19 a month for a 65-year-old woman in Dallas (75201), Columbus (43215), and Phoenix (85004). Couples can save with its household discount where it’s available, though the amount and rules vary by state.

Plan G covers the same benefits at every insurer, so compare your Mutual of Omaha quote with Medicare.gov’s list for your ZIP code before you apply.

Buy Medigap in Your Six-Month Window – Don’t Count on Fall Open Enrollment

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Apply for a Medicare Supplement (Medigap) policy in the six months that start once you’re 65 or older and enrolled in Part B. In that one-time window, insurers can’t turn you down or charge more because of your health. In most states, Medicare’s fall open enrollment offers no second chance, so don’t wait for it.

Once your six months end, insurers can ask about your health, then turn you down or charge more, unless a guaranteed-issue right applies. Major insurers’ applications list even high blood pressure as possible grounds for denial, a KFF review found. Only Connecticut and New York give everyone 65 and older another chance year-round. Massachusetts guarantees one every February and March, and Maine once a year for Plan A.

Two different periods are called open enrollment. Your six months are the Medigap Open Enrollment Period. Medicare’s fall open enrollment, Oct. 15 to Dec. 7, is for joining, switching, or dropping Medicare Advantage and drug plans, and only Rhode Island and Minnesota tie limited Medigap windows to it. State birthday rules don’t help first-time buyers either, since they only let people who already have Medigap switch plans.

If you work past 65, the window opens when Part B starts, even while you have a job-based plan, and can close before you need Medigap. When that plan pays before Medicare, as it does at employers with 20 or more workers, you can delay Part B without a late penalty and save the window for when the coverage ends. Helping a parent? Their Medicare card lists the Part B start date under MEDICAL.

Losing a Medicare Advantage plan brings a separate right, covered in our guide to Medigap after an Advantage plan ends.

Mutual of Omaha is a strong place to apply. Mutual of Omaha Insurance Company holds an A+ (Superior) financial strength rating from AM Best. Its online tool prices Plan G from your ZIP code, birth date, and sex, no contact details needed, and you can apply online. Couples can save with its household discount where it’s available, though the amount and rules vary by state.

Our Mutual of Omaha review has sample quotes from three cities. Plan G benefits don’t vary by insurer, so compare your quote with Medicare.gov’s Plan G prices for your ZIP code, then apply while your window is open.

Nvidia perps slide as market reacts to CEO Jensen Huang’s Chinese spy scandal

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The AI chipmaker announced Sunday that its board of directors is investigating CEO Jensen Huang's relationship with a woman reportedly identified by intelligence officials as a Chinese spy.

Nvidia perpetual futures slid on Hyperliquid following the AI chipmaker's Sunday announcement that its board of directors is investigating CEO Jensen Huang's relationship with a woman reportedly identified by intelligence officials as a Chinese spy.

Nvidia's NVDA-USDC perps fell 3.8% over the past 24 hours to $180.50 on Hyperliquid as of 10:10 p.m. ET Sunday, with 24-hour volume reaching $170 million. The perps began sliding around 4 p.m. ET, hitting a local low of around $178.10 before recovering slightly.

Earlier Sunday, Nvidia announced that it is launching an investigation into Huang following reports that he had been a "target of a foreign intelligence effort."

"While the Board has no reason to believe that either national security or NVIDIA secrets were compromised, the Board's inquiry will examine all available information," the statement said.

Nvidia also published an internal memo from Huang to employees in which he admitted to being unfaithful to his wife.

"I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did. I am deeply sorry," wrote Huang, adding that he supports the board of directors' investigation into the reported national security concerns and that he will fully cooperate.

The investigation came after the New York Times reported last week that a memo, circulated among top U.S. policymakers last spring, alleged that Alice Huang, a Chinese businesswoman, was recruited by China's Ministry of State Security to gather intelligence on Nvidia's founder.

"Today's news will not slow Nvidia. Jensen is committed to continuing to lead Nvidia as CEO without interruption," a company spokesperson told The Block.

On Polymarket, a prediction market indicated a 16% chance that Huang would announce his resignation or be removed by Dec. 31, 2026. A similar market on Kalshi put the odds of his departure by Jan. 1, 2028, at 18%.

Meanwhile, bitcoin Bitcoin (BTC) fell 0.8% to $75,113, while ether Ethereum (ETH) lost 4.2% to trade at $2,140, according to The Block's price page.

Stock futures, however, edged higher late Sunday. The S&P 500 futures rose 0.6%, while the Nasdaq 100 edged up 0.7%.

Asian equities advanced on Monday morning. The Taiwan Weighted Index rose 5.4%, while Japan's Nikkei 225 rose 3.2%. South Korea's Kospi gained 1.7%.

Dan Khus, chief analyst at LVRG Research, told The Block that the slide in Nvidia perps "reflects a classic risk-off reaction," with the drop "likely amplified by leveraged positioning."

"Bitcoin's modest decline suggests limited direct spillover, while Ether's sharper fall points to broader altcoin sensitivity," Khus said.

Rick Maeda, head of markets at Laevitas, said that with U.S. cash markets shut, NVDA perpetual futures on crypto platforms such as Hyperliquid were the only place for price discovery, so "it took the first reaction on thinner liquidity."

"However, the overhang will last as long as the investigation does and the real risks are congressional escalation, tighter scrutiny of China-linked sales, or any sign of a leadership change, given how central Huang is to Nvidia’s story," Maeda added.

Meanwhile, Dominick John, analyst at Zeus Research, said the decline in Nvidia per "signaled weakening near-term sentiment and increased bearish positioning."

"Deeply negative funding suggests short positioning may be becoming crowded, leaving room for a relief rebound if underlying U.S. equities stabilize," said John.

Losing Your Medicare Advantage Plan Gives You a Guaranteed Right to Medigap

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

If your Medicare Advantage plan is ending, you have a limited-time right to buy a Medicare Supplement (Medigap) policy without being turned down or charged more for your health. Use it by returning to Original Medicare instead of joining another Advantage plan, and apply before your old plan ends so your coverage has no gap.

Your plan has to mail you a non-renewal notice at least 90 days before it ends, so by early October. In July 2026, one of the largest Medicare Advantage insurers told investors its 2027 plan exits would affect about 600,000 members.

Outside your one-time Medigap open enrollment window, federal rules let insurers ask about your health, then turn you down or charge more. A plan exit flips that. If your Advantage plan leaves Medicare or your area, or you move out of its service area, insurers must sell you a policy, cover pre-existing conditions, and can’t charge more because of your health.

The right covers Plan G if you became eligible for Medicare in 2020 or later, Plan F if earlier, but not Plan N. To claim it:

The right lasts until 63 days after your old plan ends, but applying late leaves a gap. Leaving Advantage by choice? Get approved for Medigap before you drop your plan.

Mutual of Omaha is a strong place to start. AM Best rates Mutual of Omaha Insurance Company A+ (Superior). Its online tool prices Plan G from your ZIP code, birth date, and sex, with no contact details required, and you can apply online. Couples leaving the same plan can each save with its household discount where it’s available, though the amount and rules vary by state. Our Mutual of Omaha review has sample quotes from three cities.

Get that quote the day your notice arrives, and since every Plan G pays the same benefits, compare it with Medicare.gov’s list for your ZIP code. Pass on this right, and in most states a later switch means answering health questions.

Each JMWH token on the platform equals one megawatt-hour of electricity backed by energy contracts. Corporate buyers get consumption tracking, billing and cost simulations for both renewable and thermal supply. In Brazil, the central bank is reviewing Ripple’s application to operate as a virtual asset service provider, while Banco Genial has integrated Ripple Payments for USD transfers and stablecoin support. Mutual of Omaha Medicare Supplement Review – A Leading Insurer With A+ Ratings

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Choose Mutual of Omaha for your Medicare Supplement plan if you want a large, long-established insurer and can use its household discount where it’s available. Because every Plan G pays the same benefits, compare your quote on Medicare.gov before you enroll.

Original Medicare leaves you exposed. You owe the Part A hospital deductible, $1,736 per benefit period in 2026, plus 20% of most Medicare-approved Part B costs, and there’s no yearly limit on what you can spend. A Medigap policy, the private Medicare Supplement Insurance that fills those gaps, works the same way at every insurer in most states because the lettered plans are standardized. That leaves three things to compare between companies: the premium and how it’s priced, the discounts and extras that come with the policy, and the financial strength of the insurer.

Mutual of Omaha Medicare Supplement Insurance comes from a company founded in 1909 in Omaha, Nebraska, as Mutual Benefit Health & Accident Association. Medicare Supplement is still its largest product by revenue. In April 2026, the rating agency AM Best called Mutual of Omaha a leading Medicare Supplement insurer and affirmed an A+ (Superior) financial strength rating for Mutual of Omaha Insurance Company, the parent company. Mutual of Omaha has also been accredited by the Better Business Bureau since 1940 and holds an A+ BBB rating.

With the benefits fixed by federal law, Mutual of Omaha stands out for its household discount where available, its extra programs, and the financial strength behind it.

The lineup varies by state. In Dallas, Columbus, and Phoenix, Mutual of Omaha’s quote tool offered four plans to a 65-year-old new to Medicare in 2026. Plan F, and High-Deductible Plan F where it’s sold, remain open only to people eligible for Medicare before 2020.

Your premium depends on your ZIP code, age, sex, and household. Mutual of Omaha’s quote tool showed these monthly prices on Sept. 23, 2026, for a 65-year-old woman and man, before any discount:

Because every Plan G pays the same benefits, compare these quotes with the Plan G prices Medicare.gov lists for your ZIP code before you choose.

Pricing methods differ by state. Medicare.gov lists Mutual of Omaha’s Plan G with attained-age pricing in Texas and Ohio, which bases the premium on your current age, so it adjusts as you get older. In Arizona it’s issue-age pricing, which sets the rate by your age when you buy.

Where it’s available, the household discount can do the most for your bill. It lowers your premium if you live with your spouse or have shared a home with another adult for a year or longer, and the amount and the rules vary by state. In Ohio, the person you live with must also have or apply for a Mutual of Omaha Medigap policy. On our Sept. 23, 2026, quote for a 65-year-old woman in Dallas (75201), the discount lowered her Plan G premium from $215.65 to $189.77 a month.

Policyholders also get three discount programs, none of which is insurance. Mutually Well charges $29.99 a month for access to more than 10,000 gyms, with no sign-up fee or contract, and adds online fitness classes. EyeMed provides a $50 eye exam and 40% off frames up to $140, and Amplifon offers reduced hearing aid prices with a 60-day risk-free trial. You also save 15% on a Mutual of Omaha dental policy bought alongside your Medigap coverage. None of the three programs is available in New York.

Your timing decides your options. Your Medigap Open Enrollment Period is a six-month window that opens in the first month you’re at least 65 and enrolled in Part B. Inside it, Mutual of Omaha can’t turn you down or charge you more because of your health.

After the window closes, federal rules let insurers ask health questions, then decline an application or charge more. A new policy can also hold off on covering pre-existing conditions for as long as six months. Some states add protections. In specific situations, federal guaranteed issue rights also require insurers to sell you a policy without charging more for your health. They apply if employer coverage that paid after Medicare ends, for example, or if you leave a Medicare Advantage plan within a year of joining it at 65.

Medicare’s fall Open Enrollment, Oct. 15 to Dec. 7, doesn’t change those rules. It’s the window for switching between Original Medicare, Medicare Advantage, and Part D drug plans. Medigap applications are accepted year-round.

You can apply online after a quote, by phone with a licensed agent, or with a local agent. If you’re switching, keep your current policy until the new one is approved, because a canceled policy may not be available to you again. And since Medigap doesn’t include drug coverage, budget for a separate Part D plan.

Claims mostly handle themselves. With most Medigap policies, the insurer gets your Part B claim information straight from Medicare. Mutual of Omaha says it processes 99% of claims within 24 hours, based on its 2024 claims across its companies and insurance lines. Policyholders can pay premiums and update details through the Customer Access website.

The company behind a policy you may keep for decades matters too. AM Best rates Mutual of Omaha Insurance Company A+ (Superior), S&P Global rates it A+, and Moody’s rates it A1. Mutual of Omaha lists all three on its financial strength page. The company that issues your policy depends on your state. Our Texas and Arizona quotes named Mutual of Omaha Insurance Company, and the Ohio quote named its affiliate Omaha Supplemental Insurance Company.

As with every standardized Medigap policy, your coverage renews automatically as long as you pay the premium, even if your health changes.

Medicare Advantage is the different path, and Mutual of Omaha doesn’t sell it. These private plans take the place of Original Medicare and cover your Part A and Part B services. They cap your yearly costs for covered services, often charge low or no premiums beyond Part B, and usually include drug coverage. In exchange, you may need to stay in the plan’s network and get referrals or approval before some care. Original Medicare with a Medigap policy lets you see any doctor or hospital nationwide that takes Medicare. You can’t pair Medigap with an Advantage plan, and if you start with Advantage and want Medigap later, expect health questions unless a guaranteed issue right applies.

Mutual of Omaha is a strong Medigap choice from a company that has made Medicare Supplement its largest business. Its parent company’s A+ ratings from AM Best and S&P Global, its household discount where available, online applications, and built-in discount programs add value beyond the standardized benefits.

Shoppers who care most about the lowest premium should compare Medicare.gov’s list for their ZIP code first. If you value a large, long-established insurer and can use the household discount, get a Mutual of Omaha Medicare Supplement Insurance quote during your six-month open enrollment window or while a guaranteed issue right applies.

Learn more about Mutual of Omaha Medicare Supplement Insurance.

Trump Reflects on Republicans’ Midterm Losses, Reacts to J.D. Vance’s Own Analysis in ’60 Minutes’ Interview

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The president offers a few reasons why he believes the Democrats gave his party a crushing defeat last week.

President Donald Trump reflected on the Republicans’ big losses last week with 60 Minutes correspondent Ross Douthat in his first sit-down interview since the midterm elections.

Douthat asked Trump for his analysis of what happened. “Well, No. 1, I was not on the ballot,” he replied. “But I campaigned hard for some people. And I think if I was on the ballot, it would’ve been a much different situation because you saw the level of popularity and love. There was love. We had massive rallies. You had to transfer those voters, however, to candidates that they didn’t like. But there’s something beyond that. They didn’t like the war. They didn’t like the fact that gasoline went up, ’cause I had it down at a low, and there’s a great dislike, incredibly, for Israel.” Related Stories TV Elon Musk Is Portrayed on 'SNL': "The Worst Thing South Africa Has Ever Done" TV James Talarico Crashes 'SNL' Cold Open, Apologizes for Texting Us So Much

The interview aired a few days after Vice President J.D. Vance posted his own lengthy and candid analysis of what he believed went wrong for his party. He outlined four things that disaffected Republicans wanted, which led to the Democrats reclaiming Congress: “End the war, focus on cost of living and inflation, put America first and change our relationship to Israel.”

Douthat asked Trump if he agreed with Vance’s analysis. “I don’t disagree with it,” Trump replied, adding: “He asked me what do I think. I said, ‘You write what you want.'”

Trump added that he does wish Vance would have included one thing he didn’t: “The only thing I wish he would’ve said there, if he felt it, and you’d have to ask him about that, but if you had to do it again, would you do it? You know, he’s part of a decision-making process. And my answer is an emphatic yes because you can’t let Iran have a nuclear weapon. I think, historically, this will be a very good decision. Is it a good decision for today and tomorrow? Maybe not. But you know, I have to go with what’s right for our country. If they had a nuclear weapon during my term, I would be excoriated far worse than what’s happened. … I’ve stopped them from having a nuclear weapon. That’s a big thing.”

Trump argued that the economy has been strong during his second term, even though Douthat noted that polls have shown Americans are unhappy with the economy. “Cost of living, inflation, and so on,” he said. “Is that all just the Iran war?”

Trump replied: “Well, it’s not really the Iran war because the numbers are phenomenal. I think it was bad messaging, maybe by me, but I would talk about it all the time. So I don’t know, how much can I talk about it? Look, the press doesn’t cover … positive news for Republicans. They don’t cover certainly positive news for me. So I have to do it myself. I have to get it out there. We have historically good numbers. We have the highest stock market in history.”

Asked what the “bad message” was, Trump replied: “We couldn’t get the word across.”

The interview also touched on Trump’s successor. Douthat asked the president if he will be endorsing a Republican nominee in two years. “Probably. Not necessarily,” he replied. As for how he decides whether to endorse a candidate, Trump said: “I don’t know, we have a tremendous bench. I mean, I have so many people that I have great relationships with.”

Besides Vance, Trump cited U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio as a “great” option and said there are “others” including “some senators that are excellent” but he declined to name names. I know ’em all, I like ’em a lot,” he said. ” So I may or may not get involved. I don’t know. Maybe I’m better off not getting involved.”

Asked if he thinks Democrats will try to impeach him a third time, Trump replied: “What have I done? I’ve had the best two years in the history of the presidency.”

60 Minutes‘ two-part Interview with Trump aired Sunday on CBS and Paramount+.

Goldwyn Bets on French WW2 Epic ‘De Gaulle’ Crossing Over to U.S. Audiences: ‘It’s the Story of an Ally’

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The first part of the historical saga, 'De Gaulle: Resistance,' was released in the U.S. this weekend, and has just sold to China.

Antonin Baudry’s two-part World War II epic about Charles de Gaulle made its U.S. premiere Saturday at the American French Film Festival in Los Angeles, drawing a seven-minute standing ovation after both installments, each running more than two and a half hours, played back-to-back to packed audiences at the DGA Theater.

The €74 million production, starring Simon Abkarian as the French general and Simon Russell Beale as Winston Churchill, marks one of Pathé’s boldest gambles following “The Count of Monte Cristo” and the two-part “The Three Musketeers,” which were also distributed in the U.S. by Samuel Goldwyn Films.

After a slow start in France, the films quietly gained momentum through word of mouth, becoming a domestic box office success and selling over six million tickets, and are now gaining traction internationally. In the U.K., the first installment, “De Gaulle: Resistance,” which charts De Gaulle’s heroic battle to build a Resistance movement after France surrendered to Nazi Germany in 1940, currently ranks as the highest-grossing French-language film of the year. “De Gaulle” has also just been acquired in China – a rare feat — where Light Films will release it in late November.

In the U.S., Goldwyn is rolling out the epic theatrically across major U.S. markets, including New York, Los Angeles, Chicago, San Francisco, Washington, D.C., Seattle, Austin, Minneapolis, Houston and Atlanta, while making it available nationwide on transactional VOD. The second film, “De Gaulle: Liberty” will be released in early December.

Speaking to Variety during the American French Film Festival, Peter Goldwyn said he’s betting that the sprawling saga can appeal to U.S. audiences even if it revolves around a French historical figure that many aren’t familiar with.

“The films have proven themselves, and audiences are audiences, whether they’re American or French,” Goldwyn said. “It’s an amazing feat of filmmaking that you don’t quite see anymore and ultimately, World War II is an easy entry point for U.S. audiences.”

“U.S. audiences have been very much enthusiastic consistently about stories about Roosevelt, about Churchill, and I think that the story of De Gaulle is relatively untold in America,” he continued, adding that the “staggering scope and scale of what (De Gaulle) accomplished as one man is pretty amazing.”

Goldwyn argued that the films’ cinematic ambition places them closer to Hollywood blockbusters than conventional European arthouse fare.

“As a film itself, you’re looking at something on the scale of ‘The Odyssey.’ It’s just really broad and massive, and yet instead of getting lost in the effects and the war itself, it really does come down to a character study which drives the story along. So you’re not just sitting there watching World War II play out,” he said.

“The only thing that makes it an arthouse movie is because it’s in a foreign language,” he added, pointing out that the French language “makes it hard for theaters to commit on that wide of a screen count.”

As such, Goldwyn opted to pair the theatrical run with a nationwide transactional VOD release, giving the films the best chance of finding their audience.

“In France it had this sort of slow start and was really discovered, but theaters in the U.S. don’t have the patience or time to let things be discovered. It’s sort of you’re in and you’re out,” he said. “So it’s a combination method.”

The distributor is mounting a single campaign for both installments, targeting not only arthouse moviegoers but also history buffs, schools, cultural institutions and younger audiences through social media. “I think it’s enhancement, especially when you have two movies of this length, you need word of mouth and if you can only have a couple showtimes a day, you can only get so many people into the theaters,” Goldwyn said.

The appeal of the films also lies in the fact that they challenge the familiar American and British narratives of World War II, exposing the tensions between De Gaulle, Churchill and Franklin D. Roosevelt, played by Campbell Scott.

Baudry and his co-screenwriter, Bérénice Vila, read more than 150 books and dug into historical archives to reconstruct the general’s extraordinary journey from an isolated, virtually unknown military officer in 1940 to the leader of the French Resistance, as well as his relationship with Churchill and Roosevelt.

“They had different conceptions of war. Roosevelt, for his part, thought Churchill was being completely absurd with his romantic ideas about supporting this admirable little leader called De Gaulle. To Roosevelt, the idea of Free France was a little fantasy of Churchill’s,” said Baudry.

The director said the complicated relationship among the three leaders remains largely unknown outside academic circles.

“All of this history has never really been told. Historians, real historians who research this period and have access to the sources, know all of this, but the general public doesn’t. And the version of history taught in schools is not very complete,” he said.

Despite the historical complexity, Baudry wanted the films to be accessible to viewers with no knowledge of De Gaulle, so he organized early screenings for young students outside academic circles.

“My goal was that you could watch the film without any outside references, without ever having heard of that period or of De Gaulle,” he said.

One of Baudry’s boldest choices was casting Abkarian, a renowned actor with a career in film and theater who isn’t a bankable star, to embody the towering historical figure.

Abkarian spent roughly six months preparing for the role of De Gaulle, whose rigid posture, distinctive voice and almost eccentric behavior have long made him difficult to portray.

“I read the screenplay more than ten times. I read it and reread it and annotated things,” Abkarian told Variety. “There was nothing to change in the writing, not even a comma. The dialogue was really well written.”

The iconic monologues came more naturally. “There were the great speeches, and that’s where I feel at home because I come from the theater and I know how to approach them.”

Abkarian also transcribed every line of his dialogue into a small notebook that he carried throughout the 150-day shoot and eventually gave to Baudry.

“All the dialogue went right up to the very last page. I hadn’t planned it that way, but every page was filled. I didn’t have to use another notebook,” he said.

But the actor eventually abandoned the idea of simply reproducing De Gaulle’s mannerisms. “You can’t represent a character like this in a strictly anthropological way. You can invoke him, evoke him, but you can’t actually become him. And it wouldn’t make sense to try,” he said.

One breakthrough came when Abkarian and Baudry spent an evening recording De Gaulle’s speeches together.

“We explored how far I could go in approaching his voice, its frequency, its musicality and the way he emphasized certain words that wouldn’t naturally occur to you. I had to understand his logic, the way he articulated his thoughts through language,” Abkarian said. “I couldn’t make him speak in the ordinary, conversational way I’m speaking to you now. Between doing nothing and caricature, I had to find the right balance.”

The screenplay portrays Churchill and De Gaulle as unlikely allies bound by mutual admiration despite their frequent clashes, and Abkarian found an easy partner in Russell Beale.

“Simon is an incredible actor who has a profound understanding of great texts, and we have the same approach to the craft,” Abkarian said. “The writing provides such rich material for the actors, particularly for De Gaulle and Churchill.”

For Abkarian, Churchill and De Gaulle were united by an idealism that set them apart from Roosevelt’s political pragmatism.

“Churchill and De Gaulle were romantics. They recognized that quality in each other. That’s why they developed that friendship,” he said. “And that’s what makes their relationship beautiful, as well as mad and contradictory.”

Abkarian is less certain than Goldwyn about how Americans will respond to the films’ portrayal of their wartime leadership, but he believes De Gaulle’s defiance could strike a chord, particularly in today’s political climate.

“There may be something in De Gaulle’s struggle that speaks to Americans’ own battles, to their sense of nobility and their democratic convictions,” he said. “I think that, from that point of view, Americans could recognize themselves in the Gaullist struggle.”

Goldwyn, for his part, sees a hero Americans will recognize. “It’s a story of an ally,” Goldwyn said. “This man was very patriotic, which I think is very American. He didn’t go along with what his government dictated and said, ‘I don’t believe this, I need to fight for my country.’”

Impeach Pete Hegseth

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Mostly law professors | Sometimes contrarian | Often libertarian | Always independent

He deserves it for his role in starting an unconstitutional war and perpetrating over 200 murders.

Outgoing GOP Representative Thomas Massie is planning to introduce an impeachment resolution against Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth. Soon-to-be Democratic Speaker of the House Hakeem Jeffries says he's a "hard yes" on the idea. That makes it very likely it will happen. If not now, then when Democrats take control of Congress in January, after their big win in the midterm election. Hegseth richly deserves it, and should be convicted on at least two major charges: his role in starting an unconstitutional war and over 200 cases of outright murder.

On September 15, Rep. Massie outlined eight articles of impeachment against Hegseth (see here for his more detailed description). The two most compelling are these:

Article I - Waging War in Contravention of the War Powers Resolution of 1973 Section 2(c): Charges Secretary Hegseth with waging the Iran war despite no declaration of war, statutory authorization, attack on the United States, or imminent threat. It establishes that the war was unlawful from its inception….

Article V - Extrajudicial Killings: Charges Secretary Hegseth with replacing maritime law enforcement with unauthorized lethal military targeting of suspected drug traffickers, resulting in at least 221 deaths. It also addresses denial of quarter, secondary strikes on survivors, failure to rescue shipwrecked persons, and the creation of artificial legal authorities for the campaign.

Massie's Article V is the single most damning, in my view. Hegseth is a principal architect of the Trump Administration's campaign of "drug boat" strikes in the Caribbean and the Pacific. It has now killed an estimated 244 people, virtually all of them civilians (Massie's September figure of 221 is now somewhat outdated).

I have previously written about why these strikes are illegal (see here and here). But don't take my word for it, or even that of other libertarian or left-liberal experts, like Brian Finucane. Take that of John Yoo, former Bush Administration official, and leading academic advocate of sweeping executive power over foreign policy and national security issues. If you illegally order the deliberate killing of civilians - especially outside the context of a war - that's outright murder. And that's exactly what Pete Hegseth has done.

This would be true even if those killed were all would-be drug dealers seeking to transport illegal drugs for sale in the US. I have previously explained why it is unjust and illegal to kill suspected drug dealers out of hand and why they are not the legal or moral equivalent of terrorists or enemy combatants in a war. As GOP Sen. Rand Paul pointed out when the drug boat strikes began, "The reason we have trials and we don't automatically assume guilt is what if we make a mistake and they happen to be people fleeing the Venezuelan dictator? … off our coast it isn't our policy just to blow people up … even the worst people in our country, they still get a trial." But, in fact, the evidence indicates most were not on their way to the US and many were not transporting drugs at all. Some, for example, were just fishermen.

The Constitution allows impeachment and removal of executive branch officials for "high crimes and misdemeanors." You don't have to be a lawyer or legal scholar to figure out that complicity in over 200 murders qualifies as a "high crime."

Hegseth is also a key architect of the disastrous Iran War. In previous writings, I have explained why that war is illegal, violating both the Constitution, and the War Powers Act of 1973. Such a war requires congressional authorization, and Trump and Hegseth did not even try to get it. The war's illegality is one of the main reasons for its failure to this point, as failure to secure the broad public support needed to get congressional authorization  ensured Trump lacked staying power to continue the fight effectively after the initial strikes didn't achieve a quick, decisive victory.

Initiating and waging an illegal war isn't a violation of criminal law (though perhaps it should be). But massive non-criminal illegal acts and abuses of power are impeachable under the history and original meaning of the Impeachment Clause of the Constitution. On that point, see analyses by Gene Healy of the Reason Foundation (formerly at the Cato Institute), and prominent conservative legal scholars Michael Stokes Paulsen (here, here, and here), and Keith Whittington. There are few bigger illegal abuses of power than starting a massive unconstitutional war.

In fairness, Trump and Hegseth are not alone in starting illegal wars. Barack Obama did it, too, with his unconstitutional wars against Libya and ISIS (which, for those keeping score, I condemned at the time). Obama likely deserved to be impeached, as well. But the fact that he got away with this illegality is no reason to spare Hegseth. To the contrary, past laxness is all the more reason to reestablish deterrence to prevent future wrongdoing. Furthermore, Trump and Hegseth's illegal war is on a much larger scale than Obama's were, and has had far worse results.

The only potentially valid objection to impeaching Hegseth is that it might fail, and backfire politically. But the Iran War is highly unpopular, and therefore impeachment on that basis is unlikely to backfire. The politics of the boat strikes is more debatable. The administration will surely try to defend them by arguing the victims deserved it because they were trying to smuggle dangerous drugs. But that can be countered by presenting the extensive evidence demonstrating that many were not on their way to the US or even transporting drugs at all. Testimony from members of the bereaved families of the victims can help make that point, and influence public opinion against Hegseth and the administration.

Hegseth is unpopular on Capitol Hill, which makes it potentially possible to secure the needed 11 or more GOP senators' votes required for conviction (the Democrats are going to have 54-56 senators in the next Congress, depending on the results of still-ongoing vote counts in close races in Alaska and Mississippi). But even if he gets acquitted, the process of impeachment and trial can send a useful signal, and further publicize the administration's massive criminality and wrongdoing.

Obviously, the ultimate perpetrator of these illegal acts is not Hegseth, but Trump. The former was following the latter's illegal orders (though he likely also helped influence Trump to give those orders in the first place). But sanctioning officials for obeying illegal orders is still important. That's one of the lessons of the Nuremberg trials. And, hopefully, the time will come for Trump to face sanctions for his many illegal actions, as well. As with Hegseth, the only plausible argument against impeaching him is political, not legal or moral.

Massie's impeachment resolution also outlines several other charges against Hegseth. I think most of them have merit, but are less significant than those covered above. I will not attempt to assess these other charges here.

UPDATE: Back in 2020, I critiqued the "slippery slope" argument against impeaching presidents for abuses of power that aren't technically crimes. My points apply with even greater force to any such argument against impeaching appointed officials, like Hegseth. In their case, there is no argument they have some kind of popular mandate from the electorate.

Ilya Somin is Visiting Professor of Law at the University of Virginia, Professor of Law at George Mason University, Simon Chair in Constitutional Studies at the Cato Institute, and author of Free to Move: Foot Voting, Migration, and Political Freedom, Democracy and Political Ignorance: Why Smaller Government is Smarter, and The Grasping Hand: Kelo v. City of New London and the Limits of Eminent Domain.

China Box Office: ‘Traces of Justice’ Reclaims Top Spot as Local Debuts ‘Embers’ and ‘The Coffin Swap’ Arrive

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

New Classics Pictures’ crime procedural drama “Traces of Justice” rebounded to first place at the mainland China box office during the Nov. 6–8 weekend, collecting RMB21.5 million ($3.2 million) over the three-day frame, according to data from Artisan Gateway.

The performance lifted the multi-decade investigation epic to $103.7 million since its Oct. 1 release. Directed by Chen Sicheng (“Detective Chinatown”) and co-written with Zhang Ji and Yan Zijie, the drama stars Zhang Yi, Ma Li, and Chen Minghao alongside Karry Wang, Fan Chengcheng, and Chen Feiyu in a narrative tracking an elite public security detective solving complex historical cases based on real events.

Constantin Film and Sony Pictures’ horror reboot “Resident Evil” moved to second position after a month-long reign atop the charts, taking in $3 million in its fifth weekend. The video game adaptation has now reached a cumulative haul of $76 million since launching during the Golden Week corridor on Oct. 5.

Tencent Penguin Pictures’ road drama “Embers” debuted in third place, grossing $2.1 million across its two-day opening frame following a Saturday, November 7 rollout. Written and directed by Sun Aoqian, the film stars Song Jia as a weary crematorium worker who accidentally mixes up two sets of funeral ashes. When the families refuse to switch them back, she partners with the deceased’s daughter, played by Vicky Chen, on a journey across the country to return the remains. The cast also features Zu Feng.

Shanghai Captain’s rural black comedy-thriller “The Coffin Swap” opened in fourth position, pulling in $1.7 million over its two-day weekend debut. Directed by He Nian from a script by Chen Lu, the feature re-adapts Xin Yukun’s 2014 mystery classic “Deep in the Heart” through the lens of He’s acclaimed stage production. Starring Jiang Wu, Du Guangyi, Gu Xin, Fan Yilin, and Wang Weishuai, the narrative unfolds in a secluded village thrown into chaotic intrigue and conflicting cover-ups after an unidentified charred body is discovered.

Beijing Joy Fun Pictures’ body-swap comedy “Mr. & Mrs. Pardon” rounded out the top five, adding $1.4 million in its sixth weekend to lift its cumulative gross to $61.3 million. Directed by Shen Chunyang and written by Ma Duo, the satirical feature stars real-life comedic pair Shen Chunyang and Xiao Shenyang alongside Jia Bing and Yu Yang as an uninspired middle-aged couple forced to navigate marital strain after swapping bodies.

Mainland China’s overall weekend box office contracted to $16.4 million amid an ongoing fall lull. The 2026 year-to-date box office reached $4.75 billion, trailing the same period in 2025 by 29%.

90 Day Fiancé: The Other Way finally showed Andi Wallace and Nigel having a heart-to-heart about boundaries in their relationship, and I think it unintentionally revealed why this relationship may never work. Andi is ready for a relationship with a deep emotional connection, and it doesn't seem like Nigel is willing to do that.

TLC teased the first part of Nigel and Andi's conversation ahead of the episode, but now that we have the full context, I have thoughts. Can Nigel really be jealous of Andi texting other men, when he's seemingly unwilling to offer her anything different than what he does with all his lovers? Let's talk this out.

The latest episode of 90 Day Fiancé: The Other Way to air amid the 2026 TV schedule brought up something that I'm not even sure Andi realized in the moment. While Nigel went after her for texting other men, he defended his relationships with other women as they were purely physical with limited to zero texting beyond that. Minutes later, Andi mentioned that he wouldn't text her when he's with other women, and Nigel argued that he would rarely text anyone, period.

I personally found that exchange to be telling. Since the start, I've had trouble figuring out how Nigel's relationship with Andi is different than any of his other part-time lovers. Based on their conversation about texting, I'm beginning to get the feeling that it isn't, which feels like a major problem.

90 Day Fiancé: The Other Way fans are still on the fence about whether Andi or Nigel are still together at this point. That's been even more uncertain since the revelation that Andi had been on Tinder and was embracing her side of ethical non-monogamy.

90 Day Fiancé's Angela Deem Drops Cryptic Message About Michael When Asked About Hunt For Love

I'm now wondering if the reason their relationship feels so ambiguous is due to Nigel treating Andi like she's a part-time lover. To be fair, if she ended up returning to the United States like some believe, that feels like a part-time romance. Is Nigel wrong for treating someone that way when he can only roam grocery stores with them barefoot once in a blue moon?

Also, I wouldn't be surprised if Andi realized she just wasn't going to get the emotional connection from Nigel that she desperately craved. It doesn't sound like his part-time lovers are getting that treatment either, which could just mean that it's not something he's ever going to give a partner. These two just may be a bad match, but we'll just have to wait and see.

Love Is Blind’s Rob Reveals What You Didn’t See Before His Crash Out

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Love Is Blind season 11 contestant Rob Feeley exclusively told E! News about what led to his on-camera meltdown after speaking with pod mate Diana Perrone.

Rob Feeley is shedding light on what set him off on Love Is Blind.

The season 11 star finally explained why he had a meltdown at a group mixer after speaking with castmate and ex Diana Perrone, shouting that he was going to be "cancelled" following their conversation, which was largely unaired.

According to Rob, he didn't think it was necessary for him to sit down with her in the first place, since he was attending the St. Patrick's Day party with fiancée Michaella Giorgio.

"When I go in with Michaela, I don't need to see another girl," Rob told E! News in an exclusive interview. "I don't need to see these people. I don't need to have a conversation with Diana. We ended in the pods respectfully. I think she might have even got engaged herself at some point after."

"It's Michaella I care about," the 40-year-old continued. "I just thought it was inappropriate to put me at a table and try to speak to [Diana]. We had closure."

After their conversation, Rob appeared to become paranoid about their sit-down, shouting in the scene that Netflix was "gonna manipulate this whole thing into something crazy. I don’t give a f--k."

"I don't need to be put in the situation where I have to speak to somebody who I dated in a pod, because I chose Michaella," he told E!. "It wasn't anything where I had something specific to say to Diana."

He added, "It was more respecting Michaella than it was anything else."

Ultimately, the AI software salesman said the pressure of being on a reality TV show got "so immense" for him, resulting in his breakdowns both at the group mixer and at a bar with friends.

"It's just a lot of pressure, and I didn't know how to deal with the pressure," he explained. "I probably wasn't in the right mindset going in with some other things I experienced. Not a crutch by any means, but I tried to look past that. And when it came down to it, I just felt like maybe this isn't for me."

As he put it, "I didn't handle it the best, and I just felt like all the pressure just got to me, the filming, and I just cracked."

After Rob and Michaella broke up and filming wrapped, Rob said he spent 50 days focusing on himself—"no drinks, just work"—and 75 days of "eating right, no cheat meals," as well as getting therapy and doing cold plunges.

"I acted not in character," he added of his time on Love Is Blind. "That's not really who I am. Those couple scenes weren't who I am. I don't want to blame production. I don't want to blame anybody but myself."

For a check in on where other couples stand, read on.

Though yacht captain Blake’s romance with marketing manager Erika appeared to be smooth sailing after the pods, just one week before their wedding, the couple realized they may be in too deep due to their dramatically different lifestyles.

“I don’t want to hold you back,” Blake told Erika during the season 11 finale. “I don’t want you to be that captain’s wife on the widow walk. I don’t want to marry you and then for you to feel held hostage while I’m sailing around, gone for weeks on end. And then you’ve got a timeline with kids and everything.”

“I just think at this point, there’s too many concerns, too many questions that I can’t say yes at the altar,” she shared. “I just think the best thing to do is to leave the experiment.”

And while Erika cried over their decision, Blake had already concluded their ship had sailed.

“The experiment is done, but it’s the right move,” Blake confirmed. And when a producer asked if he ever had a love like this, he replied: “I’m not gonna answer that one…I’m feeling a little relieved, a little exhausted.”

While both Wilson and Wilnie had the times of their lives at their respective bachelor and bachelorette celebrations, the party was definitely over before they reached the altar.

In fact, they couldn’t see eye-to-eye on life's more mundane tasks, including dividing up cooking duties and household chores.

“When we got out of the pods, you got everything I promised you, plus,” Wilson told Wilnie following an off-camera disagreement, later adding, “You said it countless times, ‘I’ll do the things for you that make you happy.’ And I’ve mentioned what those things might be, but then you’ll be like, ‘But I don’t like doing that…I’m not used to doing that.’”

As for Wilnie, she couldn’t disagree more. “I don’t work in the home,” the mental health counselor explained. “I have long, emotionally draining days and whether you agree with that, when I get home, it is hard for me to always be at your level.”

Ultimately, for Wilson, there were too many issues at hand for them to tie the knot. “It’s not that you’re a bad person,” the senior compliance manager shared, “you’re a phenomenal person but maybe marriage is not for us.”

By the end, though a tearful Wilnie confirmed she still loved Wilson, she doesn’t regret their path.

Cam Halloran & Aneha Singh: To Be Determined

Once Aneha and Cam entered the real world, her hesitation to marry someone with a different background and religious beliefs —especially in the wake of her family’s disapproval—and their communication skills proved to be hurdles. And, yet, they seemed determined to scale them together.

However, once asked whether she was ready to say “I do,” the principal engineer shared that she couldn’t, telling her fiancé: “I still believe you’re my person, but I do not today. I need more time.”

As for Cam, he assured her that he completely understood. “It’s okay,” the human resource coordinator replied. “it’s a big decision. I feel like we both need to be one hundred percent confident.”

But once Aneha declared she didn’t and danced away from the altar while stressing to Cam that he was still her fiancé, he teared up watching her leave. As he later explained to his family, he simply didn’t “know what happens now.”

Shortly after, Aneha and Cam did end up meeting up one final time. And while Cam expressed that he still loved and cared for her, he needed time to process the “I don’t” of it all.

Cipher Digital Starts Booking HPC Lease Revenue as $9.1B Contract Takes Shape

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: Cipher Digital says it recognised its first high-performance-computing lease revenue in the third quarter of 2026 and extended its Barber Lake lease to 20 years, increasing contracted revenue to $9.1 billion. The milestone is about converting data-centre development into an operating business, not an instant cash windfall.

A signed data-centre lease and a functioning, revenue-producing facility are not the same achievement. Cipher Digital’s third-quarter update, released November 2, places the company at an important point between those stages.

In its official business update, the company said it recorded its first high-performance-computing, or HPC, lease revenue following the start of rent at Black Pearl. It also said the lease at Barber Lake had been extended to 20 years, raising its contracted revenue value to $9.1 billion.

The recognition of lease revenue shows that at least part of a project has advanced past development and into a billing relationship. It does not mean every planned data hall is operational. Cipher said multiple halls at Black Pearl and Barber Lake are scheduled to come online through year-end, so subsequent delivery remains central to the investment case.

High-performance computing infrastructure has different commercial characteristics from Bitcoin mining. Instead of purchasing machines primarily to win block rewards, the company develops sites intended to support customers’ demanding workloads under longer-term agreements. That can reduce some direct cryptocurrency market exposure while increasing dependency on construction schedules and individual tenants.

The length and total value of Barber Lake’s contract can help financiers evaluate future cash receipts. Still, contract value is not the same as revenue recognised in a quarter, cash available to shareholders or guaranteed profit. Operating expense, financing structure, maintenance and tenant performance all sit between those figures.

Cipher also reported conditional base-load classification for its 1-gigawatt Colchis project and early development of lateral pipelines intended to facilitate up to 2.5 GW of on-site generation for future expansion. These are large infrastructure ambitions, not facilities that have already delivered all their proposed capacity.

The central issue is access to reliable, affordable energy. Data-centre demand may be high, but sites cannot fulfil contracts without the supporting power network, cooling, equipment and permits. Companies that secured sites when they were focused on Bitcoin mining are trying to turn those physical advantages into AI capacity.

That shift has been a recurring theme in NewsBTC’s reporting on miners moving toward AI. Cipher’s results add a specific piece of evidence: its transition is no longer only a plan on a presentation slide, because some lease revenue has begun.

The next useful disclosures will show how much capacity has been accepted by customers, the revenue generated by each campus and the cost of completing construction. Readers should also check whether major contracts are concentrated among a small set of customers.

Cipher’s update is another reminder that Bitcoin-era data centres can develop into a very different asset class. Investors still need to judge whether the new use of that infrastructure will generate attractive returns after the enormous cost of building it. First lease revenue is a step forward, not the end of that assessment.

This article was written by the News Desk and edited by Samuel Rae.

‘Wanted’ Broadway Review: Historical Musical Plays With Gospel Truth

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The new musical Wanted has had a long road to the Broadway stage, stretching over a 12-year stop-and-start development process or, if you want to get picky, more than 100 years of real-life (more or less) family lore. The end result is spirited but uneven, a show wherein some very big singing nearly succeeds in drowning out needling plot contrivances and characterizations as thin as a wanted poster.

Based on the real-life sisters Mary and Martha Clarke, who, we’re told, set out in 1890s Texas to pass as white and undertake a robbing spree as a way to get the money needed to help their mother buy the cotton-producing land on which she has toiled for years. From the start, we learn that the events on stage are “mostly true” – Wanted has an ingratiating sense of humor about itself – but the musical is too often less tall tale than tall order, asking us to focus on good intentions rather winning us over with clarity and vision. Related Stories News 'I Play Rocky', 'Club Kid', 'Wild Horse Nine' Charge Into A Stacked Indie Weekend -- Specialty Preview Obituaries Sebastián Lelio Dies: Oscar-Winning 'A Fantastic Woman' Filmmaker Who Also Made 'The Wonder' & 'The Wave' Was 52

With a book and lyrics by Angelica Chéri, an actual descendant of the real Clarke sisters, and music by Ross Baum, Wanted (titled Gun & Powder in earlier incarnations) stars Solea Pfeiffer and Liisi LaFontaine as Mary and Martha, respectively, two impoverished sisters – they work the Texas cotton plantation their mother dreams of owning – whose light skin allows them to pass as white while they embark on their life of crime, staging armed robberies (no bullets in their single gun) everywhere from saloons to churches.

Their motives are pure: They take up arms only after mother Tallulah (Ledisi) is cruelly humiliated when she offers to purchase the parcel from the financially strapped white landowner, who is offended by what he sees as effrontery.

The crime spree proves almost instantly beneficial: The money comes easy, and romance for both sisters presents itself soon enough. Mary, losing sight of their original save-the-farm justifications as she becomes enamored of the good life, falls for the wealthy, white Jesse (Bradley Jaden), while Martha, holding truer to the solid values instilled by mother Tallulah, begins a flirtation with Jesse’s Black servant Elijah (Luke James).

Both affairs are extremely risky: The women must keep their true identities hidden, especially Martha, since their robberies are no less dangerous than the racial concealment. Lynching, the musical’s song lyrics occasionally remind, is always a looming reality.

With a mish-mash score unified by ever-present soaring gospel indulgences that give the vocally talented cast endless opportunity to belt, Wanted struggles to find a consistent tone, at first playing in a rather lighthearted Bonnie and Clyde meets Thelma & Louise sorta way before forging headlong (and unconvincingly) into historical revenge fantasy territory.

This weightier part of the musical feels somehow lackluster or perhaps just unconvincing, missing the desired gut-level satisfaction of long-delayed retribution, the sort of socially conscious vengeance that provided powerful payoffs in Jeremy O. Harris’ Slave Play and Branden Jacobs-Jenkins’ Appropriate, not to mention a big chunk of Quentin Tarantino’s oeuvre. The historical retribution rationale in Wanted feels tacked-on, with even the luxury-loving Mary making an about-face when confronted by a previously unknown fact about her beloved Jesse (a fact that comes as no surprise to the audience or anyone else paying attention).

The attempted shift toward a more serious-minded endeavor isn’t helped by the late-arriving additions to the criminal gang of the Black maids Sissy and Flo, comic stock characters nonetheless well-played by Aurelia Williams and Awa Sal Secka. Like so much else in Wanted, Sissy and Flo seem to have teleported from a different era altogether, a regrettable indulgence in the “sassy maid” trope that here serves to provide exposition and comic relief and use of the word “turkey” as an insult. And let’s hope their repeated insistence “I ain’t one to gossip, but…” is a good-faith homage to the great Kim Wayans, who popularized the phrase (and the delivery) on In Living Color more than 30 years ago.

The production’s tonal inconsistencies extend to Emilio Sosa’s costume design. With most of the characters dressed in some version of period-correct attire, the climactic appearance of outlaw Mary in a get-up that Spirit Halloween might call “Sexy Gunslinger” (form-fitting skinny black jeans, open collar black shirt, low-slung gun holster) might be intentional in a post-modern way, but is jarring nonetheless. Same goes for the out-of-place look designed for the muscular Bradley Jaden’s former slave owner Jesse, whose long-haired man-bun and bicep-showing leather vest seem less 1890s Texas than 1980s MTV. The less said about the come-and-go Texas accents of all concerned, the better.

Again, all of that might well be intentional, but Wanted lacks the force of stylistic vision necessary to pull it off. And if the incongruities work against the show, its opposite – a decided sameness in Baum’s score and Stevie Walker-Webb’s direction – does likewise. From start to finish, every song builds immediately to a roof-raising, applause-soliciting belt, a stacked approach that extends from the main characters to the “kinfolk,” a Greek chorus-style collection of Clarke ancestors dressed like a church choir from beyond, offering context and plot details (and choreographed movement) when the tale needs some padding.

Fortunately, the cast is well equipped to meet the excessive vocal demands, from the principals to the ringer-heavy kinfolk. Certainly no one could blame Ledisi, as the stalwart mother, from indulging in the let-loose approach, otherwise her frequent intrusions (usually as scolding apparition) into the newly live-it-up lives of her daughters would seem more killjoy than they already do.

As the outlaw sisters, Pfeiffer and LaFontaine are likable presences, if infrequently credible as escapees from grueling servitude that’s presented as one step removed from slavery. Even in the field – Beowulf Boritt’s barn-frame set design is unendingly efficient and transmutable – the Clarke sisters appear fresh and robust, their faces seeming powdered not so much for “passing” but for catwalking.

Newcomers to the Broadway stage, Pfeiffer and LaFontaine have impressive voices, and do their best as actors to put blood into thinly written characters. Wanted, as a modestly diverting entertainment, didn’t need to make history, but its failure to spin a dizzying yarn out of an intriguing bit of family lore is a missed opportunity.

Title: WantedVenue: Broadway’s James Earl Jones TheatreDirector: Stevie Walker-WebbBook & Lyrics: Angelica ChériMusic: Ross BaumCast: Solea Pfeiffer, Liisi LaFontaine, Ledisi, Luke James, Bradley Jaden, Aurelia Williams, Awa Sal SeckaRunning time: 2 hr 30 min (including intermission)

‘Wanted’ Broadway Review: Dazzling Cast Can’t Elevate the Musical’s Muted Narrative

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

One of the most beautiful things about legends and myths is they often stem from the truth. Written by playwright and lyricist Angelica Chéri and directed by Stevie Walker-Webb, the new Broadway musical “Wanted” is inspired by Chéri’s great-great aunts, Mary (Solea Pfeiffer) and Martha Clarke (Liisi LaFontaine). These Black women living in post-emancipation Texas decided to pass for white and wield their beauty and guns to save their family’s land and legacy. Unfortunately, despite the exceptional performances and vocal talent, the play’s actual narrative isn’t as compelling as it could be.

According to the Kinfolk, the ensemble that leads the audience into the story, this tale begins in 1893 in Marlon, Texas, in the middle of a cotton field. After struggling and saving with their mother, Tallulah (a phenomenal Ledisi), sharecroppers Mary and Martha are eager to finally buy the land the Clarke family has worked on for generations. However, the landowner, Mr. Billingsley (Nathan Quay Thomas), has other plans. Fueled by racist fury when the women offer a sum of $200 for ownership of the property, he snaps. Rejecting their hard-earned money, he claims the Clarkes are behind on rent and demands double, or he’ll evict them.

With little recourse beyond their pale skin, courtesy of their long-vanished French father, Mary and Martha set out together beyond Marlon’s country roads to earn money and save themselves and their mother from destitution. Yet as single women traipsing around the 19th-century American South, they become an immediate target. Armed with Ole Bessie, Tallulah’s pistol, the sisters soon discover that stealing and intimidation earn them cash much faster than any other profession. Though their criminality affords them more than they ever hoped, their carefully laid plans are thwarted when they stumble upon a saloon owned by the infamous Jesse Banks (Bradley Jaden), who immediately sets his sights on Mary.

Yet the Clarke sisters aren’t really the outlaws the play tried to make them out to be. Aside from a couple of stick-ups and some seducing and scheming, the rebel storyline fizzles before it even really gets started. Instead, Mary finds herself enamored with Jesse, a wealthy white man who has no idea she is a Black woman. Meanwhile, Martha reluctantly finds herself charmed by Elijah (Luke James), a Black man employed by Jesse. Only through their relationships with these different men does the conflict between the sisters arise, making the story less intriguing and more par for the course.

Though the narrative isn’t particularly distinct, especially since the central plot largely focuses on the sisters finding love rather than their outlaw status or the true costs of passing, the show’s vocal talent is beyond exceptional. Ledisi, LaFontaine and Pfeiffer are all phenomenal. Backed by an orchestra led by Paul Byssainthe, Jr., the play has several standout numbers, including “Inheritance” and “Mary’s Nightmare.” But the rest of the musical numbers and lyrics don’t exactly pack a punch. Scenic design by Beowulf Boritt and video and projection design by Stefania Bulbarella give the stage much-needed depth, allowing the audience to understand the texture of the time period and place. The singing itself, though, is truly the glue that holds the entire show together.

Though passing as an act of defiance and self-preservation is certainly interesting, these themes have been explored to a fault in films like 1934 and 1959’s “Imitation of Life” and books like Brit Bennett’s “The Vanishing Half” and Nella Larsen’s “Passing.” Unfortunately, what Mary experiences isn’t a new or refreshing take on the subject. Instead, “Wanted” presents her as dangerously naive and bewilderingly selfish, far from the badass criminal she’s initially depicted as.

In the end, because of its overstuffed book and themes, “Wanted” doesn’t quite know what it’s about, resulting in an underwhelming account of who the Clarke sisters were and why their story stands out in American history. Lacking true focus and direction, and leaning heavily on a rushed love story, “Wanted” works best as a musical album. As a Broadway show, however, it simply doesn’t measure up.

‘Love Is Blind’ Creator on Protecting the Cast and Why He Chose to Keep Rob’s Attack on Production in the Final Cut

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

SPOILER ALERT: This post contains spoilers for Season 11 of “Love Is Blind,” including the reunion, now streaming on Netflix.

Another season of “Love Is Blind” has wrapped up. The Season 11 reunion dropped on Netflix on Sunday, revealing that the one couple who did say “I do” on their wedding day, Sarah Williams and John Shannon, called it quits after he was caught texting with an escort.

However, the most dramatic part of the season centered on Rob Feeley and Michaella Giorgio, a couple whose relationship exploded in the weeks leading up to their wedding. The messiness began when the pair went out with his friends; after one of the belligerent boys got cut off by the bartender, Feeley then broke the fourth wall and yelled at production to “cut the cameras,” saying, “You’re cut off, I’m cut off.” He started yelling, “I’m not this phony ass Hollywood bullshit.”

“Love Is Blind” creator and CEO of Kinetic Content, Chris Coelen, breaks down the scene below and explains why he decided to keep it in the show.

“The participants are not under our control. Some shows are produced in what they call a bubble, where the participants are really in a specific location, and they’re with you 24 hours a day. That is not the case on this particular show. So when we are with them, which is not all the time, obviously we look out for them like we would for anyone, and we document their choices,” explains Coelen. “They’re adults, and they’re making their own choices, and we’re there to follow that. I think what you see pretty clearly in that scene is that the bar doesn’t want to serve Rob’s friend anymore; that’s a choice that the bar made, which I wholly agree with.”

He adds that there’s obviously a great deal of editing in the show, since you’re putting days of content into one episode, “but I think we’ve always made the commitment to show the essence of their journeys in an accurate way, and I think that certainly showed who Rob was in that moment in a very accurate way.”

After the episode aired, many viewers online feared for Giorgio’s safety, something she tells Variety was never a concern.

“There was never a moment during filming or production where I felt physically unsafe. I also feel very secure in the fact that if I did, I could always speak with production about it,” Giorgio says. “I’m not shy about the things that I need or standing up for myself, so my choices to leave with Rob were 100% mine, and there was never a moment where I felt like he would be physical with me.”

In fact, after the bar, the group stayed out until the middle of the night. So the next day, it was time for the St. Patrick’s Day pod squad mixer, which only made matters worse; Feeley had a “crash out” (his words), yelling that the whole experience was fake, claiming he was going to be edited poorly and that no one there really cared about him. It ultimately led Giorgio to call off their engagement.

“Our obligation is always just to support them in whatever they believe. Nobody is ever required to participate in the experience. They are welcome, and we see it happen every season, all the time — people drop out,” says Coelen. “Rob saying, ‘Oh, this is Hollywood bullshit,’ well, it’s his choice. The next day, Michaella was saying, ‘I don’t want to go to this group event.’ They don’t have to go. Nobody’s forcing them to go to the group event. It’s his choice to participate. Our approach is to try to provide the scaffolding for the experience, and then they navigate through that. That’s what’s fascinating to me and what makes every single season unique. Everyone navigates through that in their own particular way.”

Seasons 10 and 11 only featured one couple getting married, and, as noted, this season’s didn’t last. However, Giorgio says at the reunion that she still feels like the experiment worked because she did find love. Coelen agrees.

“It doesn’t worry me at all. The success of a particular season is not based on how many people end up getting married or staying married or getting engaged,” he says. “They get engaged ostensibly because they’re in love. That’s extraordinary. But that’s not where it stops. The true test of ‘Love Is Blind’ is with the love that they formed: Is that capable of surviving the real world?”

“What I’m really proud of is, we don’t take a position on any of that. Our job is to be a reflection of these particular people’s journeys, and in doing that, I think we’re a really interesting mirror into the society as a whole,” says Coelen. “Going back to Season 1, and moving forward, you can really feel some of the currents in our culture in terms of the things that matter to the participants in making the biggest choice in their life.”

Wordle today: Answer, hints for November 9, 2026

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Today's Wordle answer should be easy to solve if you like to play dirty.

If you just want to be told today's word, you can jump to the bottom of this article for today's Wordle solution revealed. But if you'd rather solve it yourself, keep reading for some clues, tips, and strategies to assist you.

Originally created by engineer Josh Wardle as a gift for his partner, Wordle rapidly spread to become an international phenomenon, with thousands of people around the globe playing every day. Alternate Wordle versions created by fans also sprang up, including battle royale Squabble, music identification game Heardle, and variations like Dordle and Quordle that make you guess multiple words at once.

Wordle eventually became so popular that it was purchased by the New York Times, and TikTok creators even livestream themselves playing.

The best Wordle starting word is the one that speaks to you. But if you prefer to be strategic in your approach, we have a few ideas to help you pick a word that might help you find the solution faster. One tip is to select a word that includes at least two different vowels, plus some common consonants like S, T, R, or N.

It might feel like Wordle is getting harder, but it actually isn't any more difficult than when it first began. You can turn on Wordle's Hard Mode if you're after more of a challenge, though.

NYT Strands hints, answers for November 9, 2026

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Today's NYT Strands hints will be easy if you're a chatterbox.

Strands, the New York Times' elevated word-search game, requires the player to perform a twist on the classic word search. Words can be made from linked letters — up, down, left, right, or diagonal, but words can also change direction, resulting in quirky shapes and patterns. Every single letter in the grid will be part of an answer. There's always a theme linking every solution, along with the "spangram," a special, word or phrase that sums up that day's theme, and spans the entire grid horizontally or vertically.

By providing an opaque hint and not providing the word list, Strands creates a brain-teasing game that takes a little longer to play than its other games, like Wordle and Connections.
